TUESDAY, APRIL 19, 1966 HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES, COMMITTEE ON FOREIGN AFFAIRS, Washington, D.C. 

The committee met, in executive session, at 10:25 a.m., in room 2172, Rayburn Building, Hon. Thomas E. Morgan (chairman) presiding.

The committee meets this morning in a continuation of the hearings on H.R. 12449 and H.R. 12450, the two foreign aid bills.

Our witness this morning is the Honorable Henry J. Kuss, Jr., Deputy Assistant Secretary of Defense (ISA) for International Logistics Negotiations. 

Mr. Kuss, I understand you have a prepared statement. You may proceed, sir.

STATEMENT OF HON. HENRY J. KUSS, JR., DEPUTY ASSISTANT SECRETARY OF DEFENSE (ISA) FOR INTERNATIONAL LOGISTICS NEGOTIATIONS 

Mr. Kuss. Thank you very much, Mr. Chairman.

I am indeed grateful for the opportunity to appear today before this committee on the foreign military sales program. This marks the third time I have appeared before this committee to review the foreign military sales program and it also marks the 10th year since I worked with this committee to establish the embryo of the revolving credit fund which is an important element of the total sales program. 

Over these years and more particularly in the last few years legislation on foreign military sales has resulted in the development of a sales tool that we have found to be quite capable in facilitating the kinds of arrangements for individual and collective security required to promote the peace of the world and the foreign policy, security, and general welfare of the United States. 

The general theme or setting for today's presentation is contained in the President's message to Congress on February 1, 1966, wherein he spoke of the more and more extensive use of this new tool to assist peoples of the world in their efforts toward internal and external security. The President said we will shift our military aid programs from grant to sales whenever possible and without jeopardizing our security interests or programs of economic development. He noted that military sales now exceed the dollar volume of the normal grant aid program. He commented that this not only means a substantial 477

favorable impact on the balance of payments but it also demonstrates the willingness of our allies to carry an increasing share of their own defense costs.

Thus, the sales tool has become increasingly a major tool of Government diplomacy in the area of international security. It is a tool that results in about $112 billion of sales activity annually as part of much broader efforts between our Government and other governments to promote our mutual security.

There have been some who view the word "sales" solely in commercial terms and therefore I thought I might comment a bit on this aspect of the program. It is certainly true that over $9 billion of orders and commitments will result in dividends for stockholders and over 1 million man-years of employment for labor. These are important aspects of the program but not the most significant. The more significant aspect is the relationship which these sales have had to our foreign affairs. They constitute a very small percent, perhaps less than 5 percent, of total military production and development in the United States-on the other hand, they constitute 15 to 25 percent on the average and sometimes 50 to 75 percent of defense expenditures of some of the friendly countries with whom our Government is associated in the world. This means that while the foreign military sale may be very peripheral to our commercial interests it is decidedly anything but peripheral in the eyes of the purchasing country and is treated in the administration of our foreign affairs with this perspective in mind. 

Military sales are equally small in terms of our total annual defense spending, accounting for less than 4 percent of the total. However, at the same time, receipts from military sales account for about one-half of the deployment costs of our forces, measured in balance-of-payments terms. The ability of this country to follow a forward strategy is heavily influenced by the balance-of-payment costs attributable to such a strategy. Thus, foreign military sales are of major interest to the Nation because they facilitate arrangements from our security throughout the world.

Working largely on a government-to-government basis in the foreign military sales field under the authority of the Foreign Assistance Act, well over 90 percent of the effort has been concentrated in 10 countries over the last 4 years. What this really means is that our major sales are to those countries who are principally involved in carrying out the purpose of promoting peace and security. 

This includes German forces who have bought over $3 billion from us in the last 4 years and who will in time of war actually make up, together with our five divisions in Europe, one of the major field armies defending NATO. 

The United Kingdom, who, with the signing of the recent F-111 program, is expected to spend over $2 billion in the United States for military arms in future years and who constitutes the principal European nation maintaining with the United States worldwide responsibility for the maintenance of peace-from Germany to the 

Atlantic Ocean to the Indian Ocean to Malaysia.

Australia, a nation alive to the problem of preparedness in southeast Asia and the need to provide for its defense has made arrangements for the purchase of almost $800 million of military products from the United States.

Italy, Canada, Belgium, and other NATO missions who constitute a major link in our worldwide forward strategy, purchased almost another billion dollars of products to strengthen their defense. 

Japan, conscious of its self-defense responsibilities, in that context of a growing national product, is expected to purchase several hundred million dollars of defense products.

As you see, the significance of these sales is great to the United States in terms of military cooperation. They represent a relationship military-to-military, a relationship that manifests itself in cooperation and logistics through the entire defense establishment of the free world.

This point should really be understood in more specific terms to understand the relationship of foreign military sales to the foreign policy, security, and general welfare of the United States. 

As I have already mentioned with respect to Germany, our program is part of a very carefully worked out set of international activities between the German armed forces and the American armed forces. 

These activities are of benefit to the equipment, logistics, and training readiness of the German armed forces, as well as being of benefit to both nations in making it economically feasible for the United States to deploy a large force in the forward area. It is a program that manifests itself in carefully planned meetings of military and civilian staffs at all levels of Government-integrated lines of logistics communication-integrated lines of voice and digital communications throughout our entire depot supply system-cooperation in research and development-joint use of training facilities and depots wherever possible the joint development of weapons like the main battle tank of 1970 and even the undertaking of problems like the metric-versus-inch system way ahead of its time, as part of that tank. program. 

In the United Kingdom the foreign military sales program was a program worked out in direct response to the United Kingdom's needs to put forth a defense establishment within an economic belttightening program. The three recent aircraft programs were part of an action to save well over $1 billion in the British defense budget. 

Without this action the British armed forces would have been considerably more restricted in manifesting a British defense responsibility throughout the world. This was a program that represented our national interest in world affairs, as well. Its solution in the context of international politics required the most astute exercise of diplomacy under the objectives and authority of the Foreign Assist ance Act. 

In Australia our military export program was first a manifestation of close United States-Australian military-to-military interests. Secondly, it was a result of the growing Australian recognition of the severity of the southeast Asian problem and, thirdly, a cooperation in broadest field of international finance utilizing the foreign military sales program tool.

Our program in Canada was and is a demonstration of the reciprocity required between two nations whose economies are so closely linked. Without such recognition the Canadian armed forces could not gain the benefits of the Canadian-American defense common market.

In Iran, sales agreements were negotiated in such a way that they were as much a recognition of the need for military strength against the Communist threat as they were a recognition of the need to maintain the expenditure of resources on military activity within certain reasonable financial levels-levels that did not interfere with the economic and social progress of each country's program. I would like to note particularly the arrangement with Iran is conditional to the improving financial condition of the country. As part of our undertaking with them the Government of Iran has assured us that the program of military purchases will not cause undue strain on their foreign exchange reserves nor jeopardize their plans for economic and social development. 

It was further agreed that representatives of both countries would meet not less frequently than once a year to assess the general progress: and the balance of payments and budget impacts. 

Similarly, our examination of the future military sales in Latin America involves as much a question of the relationship to the entire success of the Alliance for Progress as it does individual military sales. As Mr. McNamara noted the other day, we spend more of our time in places like this not selling than selling. 

In the Middle East our actions are directed toward maintaining a reasonable stability through the provision of limited arms designed to promote internal security and legitimate self-defense. It should be noted that the introduction of nearly $2 billion in Soviet bloc arms poured into the area has contributed toward altering its arms balance and stability.

The United States wants to avoid contributing to an arms race in the Middle East and becoming a major supplier in the area. Accordingly, the United States reserves the right to consider limited sales of defensive equipment to friendly states in the Middle East, but the "case-by-case" principle must be maintained. Every effort will be exerted to prevent both nuclear proliferation and the introduction of other sophisticated weapons systems into the area that may jeopardize the delicate balance of power. 

Thus you see the many ways and means by which foreign military sales program activity-carried out under the authority of Congressadministered in close coordination between the Departments of State and Defense becomes an instrument of international affairs to accomplish the objectives of mutual security.

No major change in these authorities is requested this year. These authorities are, however, placed in a separate chapter 3 of the bill which clearly indicates the task of sales as that of advancing the overall objectives of the act by facilitating the acquisition on a reimbursable basis of Defense articles and Defense services by friendly countries having sufficient wealth to maintain, utilize effectively, and equip their own military forces wholly or in part without grant assistance. 

In addition there has been introduced in the Senate a proposal to add to the objectives of the chapter two statements. The first concerns arms control and states that "the chapter should be administered so as to encourage regional arms control and disarmament agreements and so as to discourage arms races especially among less welldeveloped countries." The second statement concerns financial ability. 

It states that in the administration of this chapter with respect to less well-developed countries, special account should be taken of the coun-

try's ability to pay and the record of the armed forces of such country with respect to civilian constitutional government. 

I should like to assure this committee that this is precisely the way in which we have tried to utilize the foreign military sales tool and the way in which we intend to administer the act in the future. 

I have with me today Mr. Forman, who is Assistant General Counsel for International Affairs, and who has worked with me for this full 10 years in developing the foreign military sales program tool. 

In addition, Mr. Forman and his too few assistants spend a great deal of their time with my key assistants in negotiating specific international agreements on sales. I suppose that between Mr. Forman's staff and my own that well over 1,000 days were spent overseas last year in negotiating and consummating the kind of government-togovernment agreements that I spoke of earlier. Mr. Forman and I would be glad to provide any additional information on the foreign military sales program that you wish.

Mr. Kuss. The process of supplying military material to other governments as far as we are concerned has never been a commercial process in the Department of Defense. It has been a process of the logistics organizations in the Department of Defense, G-4, and J-4 is participating in logistic activities with other governments. This was done for many years under the foreign aid program, under a program of aid to people.

When we moved into a period here of developing a tool that did not have the connotation of aid, was not directly associated with grant aid, the process in the Department of Defense was developed through the logistics organizations who were having international communication with the other armed services. 

Therefore the name associated with international logistics activity was the general name given to this type of activity. 

Mr. HAYS. That ought to be confusing enough to confuse anybody. 

Let's not go any further.

Chapter 3 of the bill appearing on pages 24 to 33 of the bill deals with military sales and related matters. Would you indicate or have counsel indicate just what new authority is contained in chapter 3 that you do not now have? I don't want to leave you under any misapprehension, but I know or I can find out, but I want to see what you say and how it corresponds with the facts. 

Mr. FORMAN. There are three amendments in the bill. Section 

43(b), which is on page 20 of the bill, contains two changes to the revolving fund for financing sales. One change provides for the transfer to the fund of existing unliquidated balances of new obligational authority, which has been appropriated in the past, to the revolving fund. At the present time those balances are still carried after obligation in the parent military assistance account. They do not show up in the revolving fund.

The second change is to authorize the President to take the money which is allocated for financing sales in the military assistance program appropriation which has not been obligated and put that directly into the act.

The purpose of both of these changes which were recommended by the Bureau of the Budget is to enable the President's budget document to show a more complete picture of the program of foreign military sales and the status of the accounts, since these items do not now show up. You don't now see in the account itself the amount which has already been financed out of military assistance program new obligational authority. 

Have I made that explanation clear?

Mr. HAYS. I understand that. What else have you done?

Mr. FORMAN. Section 44 is not a new authority. It is a limitation on an authority we now have. Under the act as it now stands, we are authorized to sell foreign currencies and use those foreign currencies either for grant aid purposes or convert the foreign currencies into dollars which then are used by the revolving fund. 

The new section 44 limits the extent to which we may sell foreign currencies by providing that we may do so only to the extent the Treasury determines that an excess of U.S. Government holdings of that currency will not result. This amendment is in line with other statutes which have been enacted by the Congress, such as amendments to the Foreign Assistance Act and also section 6 of the Interest Equali zation Tax Extension Act of last year. 

The third amendment is in section 51, concerning barter. Under the existing law, if we use MAP funds to finance the transaction we can take the material we receive in exchange and either use that for grant aid program, for resale to a third country, or for resale to one of the Armed Forces. 

The Armed Forces by this means-for example, should one of our Armed Forces want something that is being produced by another country-can obtain it through an indirect barter transaction as long as MAP funds are used to finance the transaction. This ties up MAP funds for the duration of the time it takes to complete the transaction.

Section 51 will eliminate the MAP intermediate use of MAP funds for that purpose and enable the military departments to engage in direct barter transaction without the use of MAP funds. 

Those are the three amendments which affect that chapter. 

Mr. HAYS. The net result of the third one is to let you sell more guns to more people?

Mr. FORMAN. No. 

Mr. HAYS. Why not?

Mr. FORMAN. It is not a question of selling more guns to more people. It is a question of permitting the military department without the intervention of MAP appropriations to obtain something that it wants in the way of equipment from a third country. 

Mr. HAYS. What does it use to pay for this equipment?

Mr. FORMAN. It uses the appropriations that are appropriated to the Department of Defense for its own purposes. 

Mr. HAYS. U.S. dollars? 

Mr. FORMAN. Yes, sir. For example, we had a case some years ago where the U.S. Navy wanted an airplane, they wanted as I recall either two or three airplanes, maritime patrol aircraft being developed by NATO countries. It wanted this airplane for research and development purposes and was willing to exchange Navy equipment for it, but didn't have the authority.

So it was done by utilizing the authority of the Foreign Assistance Act and MAP appropriations. MAP paid for equipment which was sold to the consortium which they needed for this research and development program. In exchange MAP was to get back two prototype airplanes which it would transfer to the Navy and get reimbursement from the Navy.

Mr. FORMAN. Other defense articles and defense services.

Mr. FORMAN. No. 1, we do buy from other countries. No. 2, this would not affect the volume of military sales. It is not intended to be used for the purpose of selling military equipment. That can go forward right now under section 507 of the existing law under which sales are made from the stocks of the military departments for dollars. The dollars then go back to the military department's account. If it was a sales program, it would be handled as a straight sale. 

Mr. HAYS. It seems to me what you are saying is that it doesn't matter very much whether we leave it in there or take it out. 

Mr. FORMAN. It matters only to the extent that there are some transactions, not very many, where the military departments would like to barter, as I say, an airplane for another airplane for research and development purposes, or similar purposes, or where you might have a request by a foreign government to exchange an item which had previously been purchased from the United States and needs repair, to turn that in and get a new item, and pay the difference between the value of the old one which needs repair and the repair service.

The amendment would enable that type of a transaction to go on. 

At the present time the only way you can do that transaction is for the exact item which needs repair to be repaired and turned back thereby causing delay in that type of transaction.

(The information follows:) SALE OF SURPLUS 750-POUND BOMBS TO GERMAN COMPANY 

To free needed storage space and reduce maintenance costs, the Air Force in 1963 authorized the disposal of surplus munitions, including 750-pound bombs, in Europe. This was done in accordance with existing Defense Department directives. 

In early 1964, the Air Force sold 7,562 bombs of the 750-pound class to Kaus and Steinhausen, a German company interested in reducing the explosive content of the bombs to basic chemicals for resale as fertilizer, and in selling the casing as scrap metal. The total price to the buyer was $12,736. 

The appearance of a buyer was welcomed, since at that time there was little market for surplus bombs, and disposal action would have consisted of scrapping by the Air Force which, due to the nature of the material, was costly. 

In 1965, as the accelerated military activities in SEA indicated an unprecedented usage of munitions, including bombs, production of 750-pound bombs was initiated. As a means of supplementing stocks and holding dollar expenditures for new bombs to a minimum, it was also deemed prudent to acquire bombs from any other available source.

It was learned in the fall of 1965 that 5,570 of the 7,562 bombs sold to Kaus and Steinhausen had not yet been demilitarized. The contractor was approached

and he agreed to sell the bombs back to the U.S. Government. Negotiations for repurchase were begun and a price of $125,000 was agreed upon, taking into account the considerable costs incurred by the contractor, plus his loss of expected profits. For example, he had already invested time and money in development of the reduction process, he had leased remote real estate for storage, and had acquired equipment. It should be noted that his asking price was roughly $210,000, as compared with the final negotiated figure of $125,000. 

Considering the present cost of $440 for new bombs, or $2.5 million for 5,570 bombs, this transaction was very favorable to the U.S. Government.

Almost every time that someone else drops a responsibility in some way or another the pressures are placed upon us. The British have to go through a belttightening operation just as we have had to many times. If that belttightening operation occurs because of their balance of payments, so they can pay their bills when they are supposed to pay their bills, if that belt tightening occurs in such a way that they have to drop their defense programs beyond that which they would have to drop them by purchasing from us, then it is going to be a problem for us in many ways, in foreign policy ways and defense of the world.

Mr. GROSS. Since when did we go into a belt-tightening operation or have a belt-tightening operation?

Mr. Kuss. I think Mr. McNamara has been in it since he came into office, through a cost-reduction program. 

Mr. GROSS. That includes the award to General Dynamics at Fort 

Worth, Tex., of the contract for the F-111 at a cost of between $400 and $600 million more-I said more-than it should have cost to build that plane by another firm and getting a plane that can't be used on a carrier. You call that belt tightening?

Mr. Kuss. I think Mr. McNamara has spoken of that. 

Mr. GROSS. I wonder if McNamara still has the figures in his head, the cost figures on which he made the award to the Texas firm. Aside from that, you are now disposing of a plane that is unacceptable in this country, is that right, except as a land-based craft? 

Mr. Kuss. We are selling to the British one of our firstline Air 

Force aircraft, which they are extremely happy to get. 

Mr. GROSS. I would think they would be.

Mr. Kuss. It is because it is such a good airplane. 

Mr. GROSS. Not because it is such a good airplane, but because they are not going to pay for it.

Mr. Kuss. To a military man it is important what the airplane does. 

Their Air Force is extremely happy to be able to acquire these aircraft, otherwise they would not have been able to field this kind of defense force. 

Mr. GROSS. You say in your statement that it cost your combined staffs 1,000 days overseas to promote these arms sales. Why do you have to spend 1,000 days overseas selling this stuff at bargain prices? 

Why don't the foreigners come over here and buy it? Why should we spend 1,000 days to sell them this stuff?

Mr. Kuss. I didn't say that we were selling at bargain prices. In fact, we have to sell at the cost to our Government and we have to add to that our indirect costs as well. When you sell to a person, it is important to get to know his people, his bankers, his lawyers very well so you know what you are doing in the situation. 

Mr. GROSS. Why don't you let them come over here and do their buying? 

Mr. Kuss. I find it useful when you enter into such contracts not only to have them come over here, but to make sure you go over and get to know the situation as well as you can so that when you enter into a contract where they are going to pay you back, that you have a reasonable degree of confidence yourself that you have arrived at a proper arrangement.

Mr. GROSS. With regard to sales to Middle East countries, what countries in that area are buying U.S. military equipment and, more importantly, are any of them obtaining U.S. military equipment through other than U.S. Government channels?

In other words, is there a third party in any of these deals? 

Mr. Kuss. Yes. They certainly are. [Security deletion.] Chairman MORGAN. Mr. McDowell.

Mr. MCDOWELL. When country X decides that it wants to come to you and buy 10 tanks, do you proceed on the basis of supplying them with 10 of the latest and newest tanks, and therefore place an order with the tank departments who manufacture those tanks or do you take them out of the surplus stock?

Mr. Kuss. No, sir. All of our purchases are implemented actually through our armed services. The Army implements these arrangements for us. 

Mr. MCDOWELL. I don't care who does it. How do they do it?

Mr. Kuss. If it is for a sale of a tank-normally in many parts of the world we propose a used tank or a tank that is in storage because we don't want the country spending the kind of money for military arms that it would take for a completely new tank or we don't want something of that capability in that part of the world. 

So we take tanks which are in storage, which are surplus to our needs, and we sell those tanks. However, where a country insists on a new tank, then the U.S. Army places a contract for a new tank.

Mr. Kuss. I can. I cannot give it to you here. (A classified memorandum was subsequently supplied in response to 

Mr. McDowell.)

plate the transfer or sales at that time. For the firstline countries, most of the sales are in the form of new aircraft.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Monagan. 

Mr. MONAGAN. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Mr. Kuss, I have previously stated my own concern about this program. I think all of us feel a certain amount of unease about the fact that we are in the business of selling arms anywhere, beside the concern we feel about making arms available to countries where there are potential warlike circumstances. 

Looking at the language of the statute itself, I notice, and you have read the objectives before, that the third line speaks of "facilitating" the acquisition of arms on a reimbursable basis, and so forth. 

That implies that we are taking the initiative in merchandising these weapons. It would seem to me that merely "permitting" the acquisition would be enough to accomplish the objectives that we have. 

What would you say about that?

Mr. Kuss. I would say that to the extent the U.S. Government approves the export of any military weapons through this program, we have the same positive objectives for having that equipment in the country that we had through the military assistance program, that we had through the grant aid program, except the people are now paying the cost of it themselves. 

We had positive objectives, and there are positive objectives in this act for the development of defense agreements, for facilitating individual and collective security. To the extent that we continue to have those objectives, and we continue to have them around the world, we take a positive view toward seeing to it and trying to have the best equipment, the kind of equipment that will stand with our forces, the kind of equipment that will develop closer mutual relations between our forces and other forces allied with us, to that extent we wish to take a positive and facilitating view toward their having this equipment. 

Mr. MONAGAN. Of course, the difficulty comes in deciding where you draw the line. I would not argue with you about the desirability of selling equipment as against giving it away. I know that originally this approach came up in connection with the German program with the objective of aiding our balance of payments. You have already explained today how that is carried on. Are you saying that it is only for the purpose of providing equipment that is militarily necessary that you carry on the program and that you don't

10 permit the desirability of having a good international balance of payments to come in and by itself stimulate you into becoming a merchandising operation? Mr. Kuss. Absolutely. Otherwise this program would be in the 

Department of Commerce. This is why it is in the Department of 

Defense.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Fulton. 

Mr. FULTON. I am one of those people who questions this program, especially in view of your comments in your statement, the second paragraph. The President said we will shift our military aid program from grant to sales whenever possible. 

I agree with that, but the question arises, Is it still a Government aid program when we shift to sales, or an industry promotion? On page 

2, the first paragraph:

Thus the sales tool has become increasingly a major tool of Government diplomacy in the area of international security. 

In paragraph 2 on page 2:

There have been some who view the word "sales" solely in commercial terms and therefore I thought I might comment a bit on this aspect of the program. 

It is certainly true that over $9 billion of orders and commitments will result in dividends for stockholders * *

Thus, foreign military sales are of major interest to the Nation because they facilitate arrangements for our security throughout the world. Working largely on a government-to-government basis in the foreign military sales field under the authority of the Foreign Assistance Act * * * 

and so on. On page 3 likewise you say:

What this really means is that our major sales are to those countries who are principally involved in carrying out the purpose of promoting peace and security. 

In my feeling, the U.S. Government should not be in the position of actively promoting sales of military arms throughout the world. Secondly, the Federal Government should not be the sales agent for any or all American major military suppliers. I believe that is developing a military complex that is very hard to handle in this country. 

Could I ask you to put in the record what your relations are with the sales departments of the various major companies with which you deal and with which you have had reason to make contracts or contacts for sales abroad? 

(The information follows:) INTERNATIONAL LOGISTICS NEGOTIATIONS RELATIONSHIP WITH U.S. AND FOREIGN GOVERNMENT AGENCIES 

My office serves as a DOD central control and clearance house in the management of defense sales activities. In this capacity my staff and I assist the Secretary in the establishment of overall sales policies and procedures in coordination with the State Department, the Agency for International Development, the Treasury Department and other Government agencies as necessary. Important 60-929-66—pt. 3- -6

defense sales are usually negotiated by my office under a government-to-government agreement umbrella. As a matter of policy, my office does not promote the sale of any specific military equipment, but provides the country with technical information on all eligible and releasable military hardware available for current and/or future procurement. I usually insist that the final decision on the selection of military equipment remain the sole responsibility of the foreign government, subject to any overriding political-military-economic considerations. 

Under these conditions, U.S. industry remains in the background during government-to-government consultation, providing technical inputs to International Logistics Negotiations and/or the foreign government as required and authorized. 

There is another media through which my office exchanges ideas with industry and that is through the Committee on Military Exports of the Defense Industry Advisory Council. This committee, about 25 in number, is composed of representatives from the management level of U.S. defense industries who have responsibilities for the export of military products, as well as private financing and banking institutions which are active in the military export field. The committee provides a forum for the exchange of views between Government and U.S. industry in promoting, negotiating, and supporting the export of U.S. military products. It is restricted by its charter to act in an advisory capacity only to the Secretary of Defense. (DOD directive No. 5030.31, October 2, 1964, subject: 

Committee on Military Exports of the Defense Industry Advisory Council.) 

In our dealings with U.S. and foreign industry and foreign government agencies, International Logistics Negotiations is governed by the provisions of 

DOD directive No. 5500.7, May 17, 1963, subject: "Standards of Conduct," as revised March 22, 1966, and OSD administrative instruction No. 52, December 15, 1964, subject: "Standards of Conduct." Paragraph II, A, B, and C of OSD 

instruction No. 52 reads as follows: 

"A. No person shall allow himself to be placed in a position in which a conflict of interests might arise or might justifiably be suspected. Such a conflict of interests may arise or appear to arise by reason of the acceptance of favors, gratuities, or entertainment of any kind or by any other action which could influence or be reasonably interpreted as influencing the strict impartiality that must prevail in all business relationships in which the public interest is involved. 

Favors, gratuities, or entertainment bestowed upon the families of military or civilian personnel shall be considered in the same light as those bestowed upon such personnel. "B. Where there is a reasonable doubt as to the propriety of accepting favors, gratuities, or entertainment or of attending functions or accepting other invitations of a hospitable nature, all personnel shall refrain therefrom. "C. Special treatment shall not be accorded to particular individuals or firms unless equivalent treatment is also accorded to other individuals or firms justifiably entitled thereto."

My office, in its association with the representatives of foreign countries, extends official entertainment courtesies in keeping with propriety as dictated by the occasion and conducted on a modest basis. All requests for International 

Logistics Negotiations official representation funds must be approved by the 

Assistant Secretary of Defense (International Security Affairs) and Assistant Secretary of Defense (Administration) and the Deputy Secretary of Defense. 

Mr. FULTON. Likewise, would you put in the entertainment that is being done by these companies and how much is being deducted by them from their income tax for this purpose either with you or jointly with you?

Also, how much entertainment you have to do with foreign countries in order to make these sales? Then, as far as your personnel is concerned, there is a statement of your counsel that he has too few assistants. Does that mean there will be a request for further people in your Department in the next year or two, or the counsel's office?

I would like to know the reason for the basis of decision on items such as on page 6:

The United States wants to avoid contributing to an arms race in the Middle East and becoming a major supplier in the area. Would you put in the record with the chairman's permission a statement on that? That sounds to me as if you do just what comes next and decide each case on its own. We should have some method of determining what your limits are, how you make the limits, and why you make them. Then what weaponry systems are excluded from the Middle East? 

For example, at the bottom of page 6:

Every effort will be exerted to prevent both nuclear proliferation and the introduction of other sophisticated weapons systems into the area that may jeopardize the delicate balance of power.

What are you restricting in the way of weaponry systems in the 

Middle East? Then on page 7 you state aboutfacilitating the acquisition on a reimbursable basis of defense articles and defense services by friendly countries having sufficient wealth to maintain, utilize effectively and equip their own military forces wholly or in part without grant assistance.

Do you in deciding that question have in mind that the country must have a balanced budget? And if you do, isn't it ridiculous that our country does not have a balanced budget and we require it abroad? 

Then, on page 7, at the bottom, you say that you favor an amendment that the chapter should be administered so as to encourage regional arms control and disarmament agreements and so as to discourage arms races especially among less well developed countries. 

The second statement concerns financial ability. It states thatin the administration of this chapter with respect to less well developed countries, special account should be taken of the country's ability to pay and the record of the armed forces of such country with respect to civilian constitutional government.

To me that is pure poppycock and makes our U.S. position in South 

Vietnam a peculiar anomaly. If you are going to apply that fine statement in certain countries of the world and not apply it the whole way around, it is a policy that is an off-and-on policy-one of opportunism, certainly not principle.

The point that I have made of the too few assistants was on page 8 where you say:

Mr. Forman and his too few assistants spend a great deal of their time with my key assistants in negotiating specific international agreements on sales. 

I object strongly to the initiating and negotiating of international agreements of sales on behalf of U.S. corporations who are large and who can well sell themselves.

In conclusion I would like to have a statement put in the record of your activities on page 27 of the bill under subparagraph (b) in each of those two major categories set out in the language. 

Thank you, Mr. Chairman, that is all.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Fraser. 

Mr. FRASER. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

I notice on page 7 a reference to a proposed amendment in the 

Senate and I quote: 

The chapter should be administered so as to encourage regional arms control and disarmament agreements and so as to discourage arms races especially among less well developed countries. 

You say that this is the policy now of your Department. 

Directing your attention to the first part of that proposed amendment which talks about regional arms control and disarmament agreements, could you perhaps pursue the ways in which you are presently adhering to that guideline?

Mr. Kuss. I had reference to the intent to maintain arms control. 

The Department, both in Latin America and in the Middle East, has followed an extremely restrictive policy in authorizing the supply of military equipment whether it be through Government or through commercial channels, No. 1.

No. 2, it has undertaken discussions with other suppliers in the 

Western World, France and Britain, attempting to also restrict the supply of arms in these areas. These aren't always successful; but they are attempts that you make to carry them out.

No. 3, presently in study in the Department are even further methods of establishing a regional arms control in those two areas- 

Mr. FRASER. Latin America? 

Mr. Kuss. And the Middle East.

And further steps to try to keep any escalation [security deletion] in Latin America. 

Mr. FRASER. I think it was about 2 years ago, more or less, that I was asking a witness who was concerned about the military aid program, about the possibility of working out some kind of regional police force or encouraging development of regional capabilities in 

Latin America. 

I had the impression then and ever since that until the Dominican crisis we weren't really interested in it, even though there was some lipservice given to it, and then, of course, it was too late; the political events overran the situation and the countries are now unwilling to create such a force, at least for the moment.

I would think there would be some risk that we would have the same thing happen in working out regional planning in arms levels and force levels. There was a requirement placed in the act last year for military assistance to require regional planning, but there wasn't a single witness that came before us so far that has said there has been a single thing done to implement it. My guess is that there isn't much interest in it. It is a vague concept and doesn't fit with our traditional nation-to-nation relationships. 

I make the statement because between countries like Argentina and Chile I was down there when they had one of the border guards shot, and then the funeral of that border guard. It was made a national occasion. There may come a day soon when we will feel very strongly there should be some kind of regional control on the size of these military forces, but at that time the political events will have outrun the possibilities. 

I can understand what you have said so far. You have said we are trying to hold down arms levels, force levels on our own and in conjunction with other suppliers. What are we doing to encourage regional planning? What are we doing, for example, to suggest that

1 there ought to be some inter-American agreement before we sell [security deletion] to Argentina? I am very serious about this. 

Mr. Kuss. I understand that. I am only familiar with some attempts that were made in the past. And as I have noted, usually the regional planning is the most difficult type to get together, because you have to get everybody agreed before you do anything. We haven't been too successful to get everybody to agree, all the suppliers and the countries in the area to agree. Thus we have had to substitute for that planning by suppliers for a region rather than regional planning. 

I don't know, I can't answer the question for you. Perhaps I can refer this to my colleagues in State who are particularly in the arms control area, working positively in the line you are talking about, and ask.

Mr. FRASER. Except it is the Department of Defense, as I understand it, that is specifically directed to deal with regional planning. 

The method of providing arms through sales agreements and credit agreements is an important source of arms, and increasingly so, as these countries gain the ability to buy. 

We have an enormous leverage on this. We have the leverage of not only withholding all military assistance and all credit and all kinds of sales from the United States, but we have all the leverage from the economic programs. Why couldn't the United States say, not tomorow, but through appropriate diplomatic effort, that commencing in 1968 there will be no sales to any country in Latin America which are not agreed to pursuant to some kind of inter-American mechanism. Why couldn't we do that? Why do we have to wait for events to make us realize it would have been desirable? 

It seems to me this comes squarely to you, not to make the final policy, but as an important operating part of this program. 

Mr. Kuss. I would certainly be glad to try to consider how best to negotiate that kind of arrangement. I see so many difficulties, as you point out, in having the leverage in Latin America which would keep them-force the situation to a point where they just wouldn't buy elsewhere, because that is what is happening.

Mr. FRASER. It is quite clear if they buy elsewhere you deduct the amount they spend elsewhere from the amount of economic aid they get. 

Mr. Kuss. Everybody has to agree to that kind of fact. I can see many difficulties. I think it ought to be considered. That is why we don't, we don't have any objection but our general policy is for that. 

It is not to promote arms sales in this part of the world. 

Mr. FRASER. I recognize that. I start out with some apprehension, as others have indicated, as to the role of the United States in supplying arms, but my instincts are to trust you people, the Department of Defense, except I don't find there is much innovation at the political level. Here there has to be a conscious effort to break out of the old pattern.

I am not going to vote to cut any of these authorizations, but I would do anything in my power to force some kind of regional planning and regional clearance on the maintenance of military forces in Latin America. The Middle East is more complicated, and I don't fully understand it. How do you get people-this isn't just you, but

it is the whole executive branch-to start to look for different ways to do something? 

That is not the kind of question that I can invite you to answer. 

It is where I feel the most concerned at the moment. It seems to me that when we are through 20, 30, 50 years from now with these relationships with Latin America, we want to leave behind more than simply a well-trained and well-organized defense establishment. 

We want to leave behind some new institutions which will reduce the likelihood of conflict.

Here is the way we can encourage this. If we don't use this opportunity it will disappear some day.. 

Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Zablocki. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. I have but two brief questions, Mr. Chairman. On page 4, Mr. Kuss, the third paragraph relating to Japan, you say Japan is expected to purchase several hundred million dollars of defense products. Could you amplify what Japan would be buying from the United States? We do not have anything in the book on Japan. 

Mr. Kuss. This is an estimate of the future. As you may know, they are just moving into their third 5-year plan for their self-defense forces. They have been working with us in two specific areas recently. 

One is the development of a ground environment for air defense purposes. They have a start on that program. 

The second is in the consideration of both NIKE and HAWK equipment. They are considering providing that in their country for air defense purposes. You may know there are other programs that have been going on for some time with them which will to some extent be expanded in the next 5-year plan in the aircraft field. 

They have been producing F-104 aircraft in Japan. They have been producing the P-2-V-7 antisubmarine aircraft plane. They are for air defense and submarine defense purposes.

This is the type of thing that I think will bring about an increased attempt on Japan's part to take care of its own defenses. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Dollarwise, what amount do you anticipate will be purchased by Japan?

Mr. Kuss. The best I can say here really is that over the next 5 years there will be several hundred million dollars from Japan. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. You mentioned Burma. What would Burma be buy ing from the United States?

Mr. Kuss. Did I mention Burma? I don't recall mentioning Burma. I mentioned Malaysia. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Burma is not purchasing anything?

Mr. Kuss. Not through my program per se. [Security deletion.] 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. If you would for the record. (A classified memorandum was supplied in response to Mr. Zablocki.) 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. On page 6 you refer to the maintenance of an arms balance for stability in the Middle East for stability purposes. You say:

It should be noted that the introduction of nearly $2 billion in Soviet bloc arms poured into the area has contributed toward altering its arms balance and stability.

The United States wants to avoid contributing to an arms race in the Middle East and becoming a major supplier in the area. Accordingly, the United States reserves the right to consider limited sales of defensive equipment to friendly states in the Middle East, but the case-by-case principles must be maintained. 

Every effort will be exerted to prevent both nuclear proliferation and the introduction of other sophisticated weapons systems into the area that may jeopardize the delicate balance of power. 

Are you in a position to supply for the record the type of Soviet bloc arms being delivered in the area, and also, do you anticipate that the Soviet Union will provide, sell, or grant nuclear arms to the area or more sophisticated weapons which would create further imbalances in the delicate balance of power in the area? 

Mr. Kuss. We certainly don't anticipate any nuclear program there. 

The fact that they have given or sold approximately $2 billion of equipment in the area means that we still do anticipate an unbalanced arms situation as a result of that kind of effort. This is a considerably larger effort than anything we have done in the area. 

As to being able to provide information on the $2 billion, I would like to take that under advisement. The figure itself is not classified [security deletion]. I would like to provide it to you as best I can if it is reasonable to leave it that way. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. This is an executive session. We won't leave it in the published record.

Mr. Kuss. I don't have the information on the content of the $2 billion now but I would be glad to provide it to you. (A classified memorandum was subsequently supplied in response to 

Mr. Zablocki.) 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. In your statement, the sentence next to the last on page 2, you speak of defensive equipment. How do you define what is defensive and whether or not you can sell it? If it is defensive equipment, will it remain so and be used for that purpose? 

Mr. Kuss. You have put your finger on a very difficult thing. We establish it as a norm, as you recall, so it provides us with a basis for certainly ruling out the most offensive, or the clearly offensive. There are some items however which will always be in the gray area. It puts you in the position of eliminating a lot of material they would like to buy to start with. It does confine then the problem to a gray area. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Do you think as a result of Egypt's announcement that it is about to start a preventive war against Israel, that you will be stepping up defensive sales to Israel? And if so, what type of equipment? If you would supply that for the record.

Mr. Kuss. I will be pleased to do so as soon as conditions permit. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Thank you very much.

Chairman MORGAN. Any further questions? 

Thank you, Mr. Kuss. 

Mr. Kuss. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

ROMEIC
