WEDNESDAY, APRIL 20, 1966 HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES, COMMITTEE ON FOREIGN AFFAIRS, Washington, D.C. 

The committee met, pursuant to adjournment, at 10:15 a.m. in room 

2172, Rayburn Building, Hon. Thomas E. Morgan (chairman) presiding. 

The committee meets this morning in executive session on a continuation of the hearings on foreign aid bills H.R. 12449 and H.R. 12450. 

Our witness this morning is Hon. Donald W. Hoagland, Assistant 

Administrator for Development Finance and Private Enterprise, Agency for International Development. 

Mr. Hoagland, you have a prepared statement and you may proceed, sir. 

(The prepared statement of Mr. Hoagland follows:) STATEMENT OF HON. DONALD W. HOAGLAND, ASSISTANT ADMIN- ISTRATOR FOR DEVELOPMENT FINANCE AND PRIVATE ENTER- PRISE, AGENCY FOR INTERNATIONAL DEVELOPMENT 

Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, it is a pleasure for me to appear once more before this committee to report to you on the private enterprise activities of AID. The purpose of these activities is to strengthen free enterprise in less developed countries and to increase the role of U.S. private enterprise in our assistance effort.

Today I want to review briefly the significant developments in our private enterprise activities since my last appearance before you; the changes in our authorizing statute we are requesting this year; and the Agency's response to the report of the Advisory Committee on Private Enterprise in Foreign Aid. 

We administer a range of information services, risk-sharing programs, and financial programs designed to encourage greater participation by U.S. investors, with the technical and managerial skills which they typically contribute, in projects in the less developed countries. 

Our information activities are closely coordinated with the valuable activities of the Department of Commerce. Our principal publication is "Aids to Business (Overseas Investment)." To date we have distributed over 80,000 copies of this booklet which summarizes the assistance to investors offered by our Agency. 

Our Businessmen's Information Center here in Washington advises interested firms and individuals on AID's programs, and puts businessmen in quick contact with the appropriate action officers. The center is now averaging approximately 400 inquiries per month from visitors, through correspondence and by telephone. 

Our "Catalog of Investment Information and Opportunities" is a unique collection of economic and technical feasibility studies dealing with the less developed countries. The contents of the catalog are continuously reviewed and refreshed. In May 1964 when the first index was distributed, the catalog contained some 1,200 studies. The fourth edition of the index, released in January of 507

this year, lists over 1,600 titles. We have distributed the index widely and the catalog is being heavily used. Requests for copies of the studies (or abstracts of the studies) are running at the rate of 400 per month. 

In response to the President's call for increased emphasis on economic development in southeast Asia, a special southeast Asia supplement to the catalog will be released next month. It will include, in addition to the studies already in the system, some 150 new studies which have recently been collected for this purpose. 

It will also include a current analysis of investment laws and conditions for each country.

Under the investment survey program we are able to underwrite 50 percent of the cost to a businessman of examining a new investment opportunity. We authorized 63 of these surveys in 1965, compared with 46 in 1964. In the case of positive decisions the investor bears the entire cost of the survey. If the decision is negative, we stand ready to reimburse 50 percent of approved costs against receipt of a professionally acceptable study, which we can then make available to other investors through the catalog. Between the origin of the program in 

1962 and December 31, 1965, 172 surveys were authorized. Of these, 83 are in varying stages of completion. There have been 70 negative decisions or grant withdrawals. To date investors have decided to invest in 19 projects, such as a hybrid corn seed and feed plant in Brazil, a dairy products plant in Pakistan, and a cement plant in Greece. 

The ratio of positive decisions to total decisions is about 1 to 4. The positive decisions represent planned investment of just under $50 million while the negative decisions to date represent a potential cost to the U.S. Government of $461,000. Thus for each dollar of this potential cost we can point to $108 in planned investment.

We are making a particular effort to reach firms of modest size with this program. An encouraging number of applications are from this size firm. We are requesting authority for such funds as may be necessary for this program for the next 5 years.

The specific risk guarantee program by which we guarantee new investments against the three political risks of inconvertibility, expropriation and damage from war, revolution or insurrection, has grown dramatically in recent years: 1961_ 1962. 

1964_ 1965 [In millions] 

Calendar year

Number of coverages Value 50 $71.0 144 440.0 186 426.0 486 707.8 645 954.0 

The net amount of specific risk investment guarantees outstanding at the end of 1965 stood at $2.5 billion. This is more than twice the amount outstanding at the end of 1963. Last year Congress authorized an increase in the issuing authority available for this program from $2.5 billion to $5 billion. Applications continue to increase. We now have on hand over $13 billion in applications for insurance coverage. Many of these applications do not materialize as contracts because they are filed in the early stages of project planning. However, we estimate that we will need, and have requested, an increase in this guarantee authority from $5 billion to a ceiling of $10 billion to cover the next 5 years. The list of countries that have agreed to institute this program has continued to grow-73 less developed countries have now entered into the necessary agreements. Since the beginning of 1965 agreements have been signed with Brazil, Dahomey, Chad, Uganda, British Guiana, Upper Volta, Rwanda, Singapore, British Honduras, and Ceylon. During that same period our agreements with India, Thailand, Korea, Malaysia, and Costa Rica were amended to provide additional coverages. The agreement with Brazil was announced at this time last year. The program is now in operation. Several contracts have been issued and we have on hand applications for over $200 million in guarantee coverage.

Section 620 (1) of the Foreign Assistance Act requires that assistance be terminated by December 31, 1966, to those countries that have not signed bilateral

agreements covering inconvertibility and expropriation. There are relatively few countries in which the agreements necessary to escape the application of -section 620 (1) are now lacking. It may be in the national interest to continue assistance to these countries, even in the absence of investment guarantee agreements. Therefore we propose that Congress replace the present mandatory termination provision with one providing for presidential flexibility.

Certain operational changes in the program are significant. We have compiled a statement of the policies and procedures of the program in a new handbook and have made certain changes in our fee structure. On March 15, 1966, the fee for convertibility insurance was reduced from one-half of 1 percent per annum to one-fourth of 1 percent per annum. At the same time we began to offer optional coverage against the risks of expropriation and war damage in a combined contract. The combined coverage is available at a single rate of seven-eights of 1 percent per annum instead of the rate of one-half of 1 percent per annum which applies to each of the separate coverages. In addition, the fee for standby coverage has been reduced from one-half of 1 percent to one-tenth of 1 percent. We expect these changes to improve the capacity of the program to attract additional investment to the developing countries. 

Claims against the program continue to be light, although they are larger than last year. Three new claims have been filed in the past 12 months. Our reserves are more than ample to cover these claims. 

The extended risk guarantee authority for nonhousing projects under section 221 (b) (2) authorizes us to guarantee investors against loss of up to 75 percent of the investment. This applies to all risks except the fraud or misconduct of the investor. The statutory ceiling on this authority is currently $175 million. 

Progress under this program has continued to be slow. We have authorized guarantees totaling $14.1 million for four projects. Three of these were issued in 1965 for a carbon black plant in Colombia, a fish cannery in Somalia, and a fertilizer plant in Korea. A guarantee for a second fertilizer plant in Korea was authorized last year, but canceled by the investor who decided to go ahead without it. Two major industrial projects are in the final stages of negotiation. 

There is a strong possibility that another $20 million in guarantees for these projects will be authorized before the end of this fiscal year. An additional $40 million in projects have been shown to us as formal applications or serious inquiries.

We have recently worked out with the investment community a change in the structure of the guarantee contract, which is more likely to meet the investor's needs than any form we have offered so far. The institutional investors who need full protection will have it, and the commercial lenders in the project will hold the early maturities without an extended risk guarantee. 

Last year Congress provided $175 million in authority for this program to cover a 2-year period. We now request $300 million in authority to extend to 

June 30, 1973.

Under the Latin American housing guarantee program there were 14 housing projects completed or under construction as of February 28, 1966. These projects involve a total investment of $82.2 million, covering the construction of 

16,000 dwelling units. Another 36 projects, involving $157.2 million in investment and 28,000 dwelling units, are in various stages of processing. As you know, the Agency recently announced new criteria for the expanded Latin American housing guarantee program authorized in last year's act. 

Outside of Latin America, one guarantee has been authorized for a housing project in the Republic of China; other applications are being processed for projects in Thailand and several African countries. 

From the proceeds of title I, Public Law 480 sales we made 38 local currency Cooley loans to U.S. firms or their affiliates in 13 countries amounting to the dollar equivalent of $35.4 million in 1965. These loans were for such varied activities as the production of cement in Bolivia, wire rope in Colombia, chemicals and tractors in India, paperboard in Israel, food products in Pakistan; and for the establishment of a small business investment in Tunisia. The proposed food-for-freedom legislation provides for a continuation of this kind of lending. 

In addition to these programs, specifically designed to encourage and assist U.S. private investors, I also wish to report on programs which provide financial assistance to private enterprise, whether locally owned, U.S. owned, or joint venture.

We have lent substantial amounts of local currency generated by AID program loans to local credit institutions for relending to the private sector. One

such institution, the private investment fund in Colombia, has relent pesos to over 100 subborrowers in amounts totaling over $45 million equivalent. Funds will be established in Brazil and Chile which will relend counterpart currencies to help finance imports of U.S. spare parts and equipment. 

We continue to support intermediate credit institutions as a major means of spurring private enterprise development. During calendar year 1965, eight dollar loans amounting to $46.5 million were authorized to eight institutions. 

We make dollar and local currency loans to industrial development banks, housing institutions, agricultural credit banks, and banks for cooperatives. 

The ownership of these institutions may be private, Government, or mixed but in each case they make their subloans to private borrowers. 

With regard to direct dollar loans to private borrowers, AID authorized nine loans amounting to $100.2 million during 1965. This is about the level of our lending to private borrowers in 1964, reflecting availability of funds from alternative free world sources, such as local intermediate credit institutions, private lenders, the Export-Import Bank, the IFC, and the IDB. Loans were authorized in 1965 for such projects as a cement plant and a hardboard factory in Brazil, two chemical fertilizer plants and a cement factory in Korea, and a gas transmission system in Pakistan.

Program loans continue to play a key role in private enterprise development. 

During calendar year 1965 we authorized 10 nonproject loans amounting to $371.5 million. By providing foreign exchange for the purchase of U.S. capital goods, spare parts and industrial raw materials, these loans are a strong stimulus to the productivity of the private sector. In addition, they contribute significantly to our ability to influence aid-receiving governments to establish realistic exchange rates, liberalize import restrictions, enact important tax measures, and combat inflation.

Our programs to encourage private U.S. investment in developing countries strengthen our Nation's businesses and in addition do much more. These businesses carry essential technology and know-how to places where they are most needed. Their impact expands and multiplies through suppliers, employees, customers, partners, stockholders, and competitors. As a result, AID's interest in private enterprise goes far beyond its interest in facilitating and encouraging U.S. private investment. The dollar loans, program loans, and local currency loans I have described furnish significant financial support for a strengthened local economy. However, to improve the local conditions for local and foreign private investment a comprehensive approach must be taken, and a broad effort undertaken to identify and deal with obstacles to private economic activity in countries where significant AID programs are underway. In addition to financial assistance, this brings into play a wide range of technical assistance in such fields as investment promotion, management and vocational training, and improvement of legal and financial institutions. It also points to a wide area of exchange of ideas between aid-receiving and aid-giving countries looking toward the elimination of restrictive, ineffective, or inefficient policies and procedures. 

We are already doing many things of this kind in our major country programs. 

In Turkey, for example, a new Government has been elected on a platform which included a statement of intention to place strong reliance on the capacity of Turkish private industry to grow to meet the country's needs. In response, we are engaged in projects to assist and strengthen the Turkish Management Association, the Management Consulting Center, and the National Productivity Center. We are strengthening the capacity of the banking system to extend credit on longer terms to small and medium-sized businesses outside of the major metropolitan areas. We are supporting technical and vocational education, inplant training, and labor relations training. 

In other countries different components may be present. In Latin America, for example, the use of cooperative mechanisms has proven particularly effective and continues to expand. 

In recognition of the breadth of these possibilities, an amendment to the Foreign Assistance Act of 1963 called for the establishment of a nine-member group to "carry out studies and make recommendations for achieving the most effective utilization of the private enterprise provisions of this act. ***"

The Advisory Committee on Private Enterprise in Foreign Aid, chaired by 

Arthur K. Watson of the IBM World Trade Corp., presented its report to the 

President on August 25, 1965.

The report's 33 recommendations are directed to many aspects of private involvement in the development process: Private foreign investment; stimulation of private economic activity within the developing countries; the role of business, the universities, the voluntary agencies, professional associations and foundations; the overriding importance of the development of economic resources.

Many of the recommendations are quite specific. For example: that AID expand the use of its extended risk guarantee authority; that it offer combined specific risk guaranty coverage and lower the fees; that the United States sign and ratify the World Bank-sponsored Convention for the Settlement of Investment Disputes; that Congress enact tax legislation offering incentives for U.S. business to invest in LDC's.

Other recommendations are broad, designed to influence the tone and emphasis of AID's total program. 

Action has been completed on a number of specific recommendations. The 

Secretary of the Treasury has signed the Convention for the Settlement of Investment Disputes which is now before the Congress for ratification. As mentioned above. AID now offers combined war and expropriation coverage and has reduced premium rates for its specific risk guarantees. The U.S. 

Government is broadening its collaborative efforts with other governments in the antitrust field as recommended by the Committee. Action has been completed permitting the IFC to borrow up to $400 million from the World Bank for investment in private enterprise in the member countries. The emphasis on the importance of education in development strategy recommended by the Committee is fully reflected in the fiscal year 1967 foreign aid proposals. AID and the Export-Import Bank have reviewed their guaranty policies covering exports of technical and professional services as recommended by the Committee. 

The President has endorsed the concept of binational foundations utilizing excess local currencies. Plans for the establishment of the first such foundation in India were announced in March 1966. 

Among the broader recommendations, the Committee's first recommendation, that AID make an intensive study, in selected countries, of the factors affecting the climate for investment, both domestic and foreign, is particularly important. 

Studies are underway in Thailand, Korea, Turkey, Brazil, Chile, and Tunisia.

They will provide the analytical background against which our existing programs can be tested in the light of the Committee's recommendations dealing with: investment project identification, analysis and promotion; capital loans and credit availability; legal and administrative structures and procedures; vocational and management skills. The objective is not a study but rather a longrange strategy to develop flourishing local economies. 

The report has been well received throughout AID as a thoughtful and useful analysis of the role private resources can and must play in economic and social development. It has given a useful stimulus to AID's efforts and will have a continuing influence in shaping and guiding future programs. 

In an effort to involve a major non-Federal resource in development assistance, and to improve the business management of local industrial and commercial enterprises in the less developed countries we have assisted in the launching of the International Executive Service Corps. This is a private organization, managing the activities of volunteers from the ranks of senior American business executives. During 1965 the program grew steadily and at the yearend had over 100 projects underway in 18 countries. It has a distinguished board of directors, and an able, energetic chief executive officer in the person of Frank Pace, Jr. Typical of the assignments undertaken by these executives are: assistance to the management of a dairy firm in Iran; improvement of merchandising procedures of a department store in Panama; improving production procedures of a manufacturer of general electrical products in Taiwan. This is a young program, but the early results are promising. 

During 1965 the Development Assistance Committee of the OECD decided to include private investment matters in its activities. Representatives of member countries have held several meetings resulting in a work program to exchange information on their experience in using investment incentives, to coordinate public and private development efforts, to improve statistics on the private flows of capital from all capital exporting countries to developing countries and to study the overall economic effects of foreign private investment.

The Business and Industry Advisory Committee of the OECD is expected to play a leading role in this work. 

In his foreign aid message this year, the President made it clear that most of the work of economic development must be done by the people of the developing countries themselves. He outlined major calls to action for the people of the developing countries--he identified actions which are necessary to real progress, emphasizing that they are difficult but necessary, and that "we will not continue any partnership in which only we recognize that fact." One was a challenge to "create the climate which will attract foreign investment, and keep local money at home." In a real sense, the entire assistance program supports that effort. Major capital improvements supply power, transportation, and communications essential to business growth. Nonproject and local currency lending, and many forms of technical assistance support private economic activity.

But the job cannot be done by Government alone. In carrying out strengthened programs in the fields of agriculture, health, and education, we will turn increasingly to the private resources of this country to participate. The President stated the essentials of this approach, in which we think our greatest opportunity for increased effectiveness lies, in his foreign aid message: "The U.S. Government can do only a small part of the job of helping and encouraging businessmen abroad. We must rely more and more on the great reservoirs of knowledge and experience in our business and professional communities. These groups have already provided invaluable service and advice. We in Government must find ways to make even greater use of these priceless assets."

Mr. HOAGLAND. Mr. Chairman, it is a pleasure for me to appear once more before this committee to report to you on the private enterprise activities of AID. The purpose of these activities is to strengthen free enterprise in less developed countries and to increase the role of U.S. private enterprise in our assistance effort. 

Today I want to review briefly the significant developments in our private enterprise activities since my last appearance before you; the changes in our authorizing statute we are requesting this year; and the 

Agency's response to the report of the Advisory Committee on Private 

Enterprise in Foreign Aid. 

We administer a range of information services, risk-sharing programs and financial programs designed to encourage greater participation by U.S. investors, with the technical and managerial skills. which they typically contribute, in projects in the less developed countries.

The program representing the largest volume of activity with which we deal is the specific risk investment guarantee program. This program has been in operation since 1949 and it has been growing at a very strong rate. In calendar year 1961 we issued 50 coverages with a dollar value of $71 million. In 1963 these figures had grown to 186 coverages at $426 million. In 1964 it was 486 coverages at $707 million.

During calendar year 1965, we issued 645 coverages valued at $954million. This means, for example, that the 1965 rate of issuance was more than double the 1963 rate of issuance. I am particularly happy to report that since in previous years there has been concern about the growth of that program, I feel confident at the present time it is meeting the needs of investors. It is improving both substantively and mechanically, as it is continuing to find new and effective ways to be responsive to the general concern of investors over the particular risks-

it covers, the risks of incontrovertibility, expropriation, and damage from war, revolution or insurrection.

This year we are asking for an increase in the authority of that program from the current ceiling of $5 billion to a ceiling for an additional 5-year period of $10 billion. This anticipates a rate of issuance of approximately a billion dollars a year and builds into the authority approximately-although this is necessarily an estimate the rate of growth that we are presently experiencing. The $10 billion figure is what we anticipate would be needed to carry us through the period of authorization requested by the administration bill. 

During 1965 we again made progress on the execution of bilateral agreements with additional countries considering the institution of this program. Ten additional countries have signed these agreements since the beginning of 1965. The most significant of them is Brazil. 

I reported on the signing of the Brazil agreement to you last year, but I can now report that the program has been ratified by the Brazilian Congress, that guarantees have been issued and that the program is operative in Brazil. 

Mr. ADAIR. Does that cover all three of the specific risks?

Mr. HOAGLAND. Yes, sir, it does, as well as the extended risk guarantee, the one which guarantees against all risks not just political risks, with the exception of the risk of the investor's own fraud and misconduct.

Under this authority during 1965 we issued three contracts using slightly over $14 million in authority. This authority is one that has not yet been used on a broad scale.

The ingredients to me seem to be there for a financial device which can meet the needs of investors seeking dollar credits in situations of importance to our general purpose to facilitate an increase in the participation of private enterprise in economic development. The mechanism is a complex one. It has not yet been refined to the point where it can be used in large quantity. 

At the present time we are in the final stages of negotiation of transactions using the form of guarantee that we discussed last year on which we anticipate the greatest immediate use of this authority. 

This is the kind of guarantee which would split the authority so that a commercial bank would take the risk on the early maturities and an institutional lender would take the risk on the late maturities. 

Taking this approach, 75 percent of the debt involved in an investment plan could be held by an institutional lender and receive the full benefits of the guarantee.

This would be a guarantee of 75 percent of the debt involved, but it would give the guarantee benefits to the long-term lender and it would put the shorter term investor at risk. The commercial bank, I believe, would find it in its interest to take a risk of 2, 3, or perhaps 4 years in the developing country situations but I doubt very much that institutional lenders would take any risk at all on long-term investments in developing countries under the present conditions. We have worked very closely with the investment community in trying to refine this style of guarantee to meet their needs and in the past few weeks we have worked out arrangements that would make it possible for the

guarantee to run directly to the institutional lender rather than to run to him by participation through the interest of the commercial bank, which is at risk on the early years.

This difference was in response to the reactions we had to the style of guarantee that we described to you last year and is the form of transaction on which as I say we are very close to concluding arrangements in three transactions currently before us. This does not represent a high rate of use of the available authority. 

Nevertheless, I think that the potential of this kind of authority and the importance of working patiently and ingeniously toward methods of bringing commercial banks and institutional lenders into the process of financing developing country investments justifies the request we make that for the 5-year period contemplated by the administration bill, plus the necessary period to maintain the availability of the authority after the last year of the legislation, there be established a level of $300 million of authority for this program. 

This is an annual level somewhat below the annual level that is available under the existing authority, but it maintains this program at a level which would give institutions the confidence that the program has the support of Congress, will continue to be in existence and is something that they can use in their investment plans for the long term.

The other point I wish to refer to in terms of specific activities during the last year relates to the Advisory Committee on Private 

Enterprise in Foreign Aid. This is the Committee chaired by Arthur 

Watson of IBM, which reported in August of 1965. This Committee made a wide range of recommendations, 33, directed to many aspects of private involvement in the development process. 

They dealt with private foreign investment, with the stimulation of private economic activity within developing countries, and the role of business, labor, universities, voluntary agencies, cooperative associations in the development process. 

A number of their recommendations were concrete and specific and have been adopted. Some of them were involved in the aid legislation adopted by the Congress last year-changes in the specific risk guarantee authority in particular. The action already taken in connection with the convention for the settlement of investment disputes which is now before the Congress for ratification, is in conformity with the recommendation of that Committee.

We have gone ahead with reductions in the fees for specific risk guarantees, again in response to one of their recommendations, and have made arrangements to offer a combined coverage on expropriation and war risk which pursues one of their recommendations, although it does not adopt it precisely.

Action has been completed permitting the IFC to borrow up to $400 million from the World Bank for investment in private enterprise in its member countries.

The emphasis that they placed on the importance of education in development has been fully responded to by the administration in its fiscal year 1967 proposals to the Congress.

པ་ The President has generally endorsed the concept of bi-national foundations using excess local currencies and plans for the establishment of such a foundation in India were announced in March of this year.e

There is a broader set of recommendations made by the Committee which can't be responded to quite as concretely and specifically as that. They have in general urged us to pursue tax incentives. We accept that idea and are examining several alternatives. 

They have in their first recommendation urged us to make intensive studies in selected countries of all of the factors affecting the climate for investment for local and foreign investment. We are now engaged in doing that in Thailand, Korea, Turkey, Brazil, Chile, and

Tunisia. This approach is reflected as well in several other recommendations that encourage us to undertake a broader program of feasibility studies, to improve the legal and financial institutions that support private investment, and to undertake additional programs for management education, and to transfer technical training and technology to the developing countries through the use of the skills in our own private sector.

We agree with the general principle of those recommendations. We feel we have been doing a considerable amount of work of this kind, but we accept the spur this gives us to do a better job and to analyze more carefully, evaluate more thoroughly the programs of this kind that we are carrying on.

The general content of the AID foreign enterprise activity should I propose, be viewed not solely as support for U.S. private enterprise going abroad, but as support for the strengthening of the concept of private enterprise and the strengthening of the private sectors in the countries in which we work, and as one of the major facets of a comprehensive and promising aid program. 

Gesident has made it clear in his foreign aid: that he considers that the efforts of a country to improve the climate for foreign investment and to take those steps necessary to keep its own capital at home and effectively employed are to be considered as explicit and significant parts of the self-help performance of a country that we should examine in order to determine whether it make sense for us to have significant aid programs in the country. 

Finally, he made it clear that in all of the aspects of our foreign assistance work the U.S. Government can do only a small part of the job. We must rely more and more on the reservoirs of knowledge and experience in our own business and professional communities. 

These groups have already provided invaluable service and advice. 

We in Government must find ways to make even greater use of these priceless assets. This is a theme that we pursue with all of the energy we can. our I think there are many aspects of the aid I program that can be made more effective by an improvement in contacts with and use of American private resources. We look to a greater emphasis on that effort in coming years,

I would be glad to deal at this point with any questions you may have, Mr. Chairman. nol daull Freqorl 60-929-66-pt. 3-

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mr. Hoagland. 

Mr. Hoagland, I understand that a situation has arisen which tends to discourage the use of the investment guarantee program by U.S. investors where part of the financing for the oversea projects might be financed by the Export-Import Bank. It is my impression that the 

Export-Import Bank wants a U.S. investor to assign some of its protection under the investment guarantee program to the bank. 

It is not my understanding that the investment guarantee program was intended to guarantee investments of U.S. Government agencies. 

I thought it was to apply strictly to private investments by U.S. investors. Will you discuss this problem for the record? Are you aware of it?

Mr. HOAGLAND. I am aware of this problem, Mr. Chairman. This is a complicated issue and one in which in effect it is necessary to consider what the intention of Congress may have been in situations of this kind. The situation involved would be one in which the Eximbank is making a loan to a proposed project sponsored by a private 

American firm.

That firm would be a stockholder-let's assume it might be the only stockholder in the company receiving the loan. The investor would come to AID for guarantee protection against losses from risks such as expropriation and if there had been no Export-Import Bank loan in the picture, we would have proceeded to offer this coverage in an amount equal to the amount invested, plus earnings of the operation abroad if they are kept in the project up to an amount equal to the original amount of the investment.

This is to encourage reinvestment and Congress made a specific amendment in the statute to permit us to issue the guarantees to cover not only the original investment but also earnings that are kept in the project.

The Eximbank approaches this subject naturally from the point of view of its own responsibilities and is concerned that it is inappropriate for a situation to arise in which an investor might be compensated and come away from a project which has collapsed as a result of some such calamity of this kind, not just with his investment, but with substantial profits represented by these earnings while the U.S. 

Government suffers a loss because the Export-Import Bank had a loan in the project which is not paid off. 

The thing we have to remember here is that our guarantee insurance goes to a stockholder, an investor, but not to the company in which the investment is made. So that when we pay off on an expropriation guarantee, our money goes to the investor and it isn't therefore in the hands of somebody who owed it to the Export-Import Bank. The investor might go away with his investment, while the Export-Import 

Bank suffers a loss.

As we see our responsibilities, we were supposed to be guaranteeing investors against expropriation and our authority should be used the same way whether there is an Export-Import Bank loan or not. 

The Eximbank feels it is inappropriate for this to happen and that the United States should not suffer a loss through the Export- Import Bank loan default when the investor makes a profit. We are

discussing this matter at the present time and it is a matter on which an expression of the intention of Congress would be helpful to us. 

You might want to request a statement for the record from the Export- 

Import Bank on this matter. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Hoagland, on page 5 of your statement you say that there are relatively few countries in which the agreements necessary to escape the application of section 620 (1) are now lacking.

I know Mr. Adair has been interested in this for many, many years.

Mr. HOAGLAND. I have a list of the countries where the investment guarantees are not available for the coverages necessary to comply with the amendment that you refer to. I would not characterize these as countries that do not want to sign the agreement in all cases. 

Many of these are smaller African countries with which it takes a considerable period of time to negotiate this and where I think there is no real resistance. The African countries in which it has not been signed would include Algeria, Libya, Cameroon, Burundi, Malawi, and Zambia. You can see in that list countries such as Zambia where assistance may be desirable and where we feel we are making good progress toward the execution of the agreement. 

They have not been independent for very long. We have other interests in the country which it seems to me would cause us to want to be able to continue an aid program under current circumstances. 

Elsewhere in the developing countries we would have countries like Yemen and Lebanon where we do not yet have this agreement. We would have other countries where investment is highly unlikely in which we don't have the agreement. 

The country that I think would concern us most might well be Peru because there our national interest is considerable. I think we are making good progress toward the nogiation of an agreement in Peru. 

It is under active negotiation. 

I can and will furnish the complete list of the countries. I have already named more than half of them.

(The information follows:) ARTHUR D. LITTLE, INC., CONTRACT-NIGERIA 

Expansion of investment in Nigerian industry is an important part of the nation's 6-year plan. During the period 1963 through 1965 over $300 million worth of new industrial investment has been made in Nigeria. Counting projects which are presently planned and likely to go ahead this figure may be increased by another $100 million. 

Since Arthur D. Little, Inc. Additionally in 1963, $68 million was invested in 14 new plants still under construction at yearend, and $118 million invested for expansion of 22 existing facilities as well as $33 million in planned investment still in the blueprint stage. Mrs. BOLTON. What has the effect been of these coups in Africa on private investment? Has it fragmented away or has it given them a feeling that perhaps things are steadying down? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. The immediate effect certainly is disruptive and increases people's apprehension. But in the specific instances, the new governments in almost all cases have been people who have seemed to have been taking a better attitude toward the use of private investment than the people they replaced. 

In Nigeria the new government seems to be intent on putting the house in order and welcoming private investment. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Are they eliminating some taxes? Are they making it easier to invest in Nigeria?

Mr. HOAGLAND. I am not sure they are eliminating taxes. I think all I can report so far are the things that they have been saying. I don't think I can report specific actions, but our evaluation of them and their statements show a desire to eliminate corruption and improve the administration of the ministries that have to deal with private investment.

Mrs. BOLTON. What about Ghana? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. This also sounds promising. The new government sounds very different from the old.

(The information follows:) LOANS ADMINISTERED BY AID IN DELINQUENT STATUS 

At December 31, 1965, 54 loans with an outstanding balance of $269.1 million were in delinquent status. Payments of $7.3 million in principal and $6.3 million in interest were overdue. By April 30, 1966, 29 of these loans had been cleared either through repayment or by reason of deferral. Principal payments cleared during this period amounted to $855,129, interest payments to $883,609. 

Mrs. BOLTON. How many loans have been converted to grants because of the inability of the borrower to meet the loan? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. I would be glad to furnish that also. 

(The information follows:) 

LOANS CONVERTED TO GRANTS 

Of the loans administered by AID, only two have been converted to grants. 

In one case a loan of $1 million was made to an American charitable organization, of which $30,000 was repaid and the balance of $970,000 was converted to a grant on February 29, 1964, because of inability of the borrower to meet the loan. In another case, $1,366,300 of a total loan of $3,500,000 to a foreign government, was converted to a grant on March 7, 1964, on the basis that this amount represented excess costs arising from the use of U.S. construction contractors. 

Mrs. BOLTON. How many direct private loans since last year?

Mr. HOAGLAND. I believe there were nine, amounting to approximately $100 million in lending. 

Mrs. BOLTON. In what countries were they? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. There were four in Brazil, two in Korea, excuse me, three in Korea, one in Pakistan, and one in Mexico.

Mr. HOAGLAND. It relates really to some unpredictable factors.

projects of this kind. So that frequently the opportunity for us to finance a dollar need arises where the Export-Import Bank for some reason is not available.

(The information follows:) SPECIFIC RISK GUARANTEES 

Reconciliation.-Chart: Investment guarantees total amount in force, 196165 (p. 31, presentation book) and dollar value of contracts written, 1961-65 (p. 4, Donald W. Hoagland statement).

The increases in the amounts of specific risk investment guarantees outstanding at the end of each calendar year, shown in the chart, are less than the amounts of new guarantee coverage written for each of the years as indicated on page 4 of the statement. The principal reason for the variance is the fact that the yearend amounts reflect reductions and terminations of existing contracts which had taken place during the year while the amounts of new guarantee coverage written do not. Other factors which contribute to the difference, although to a lesser degree, stem from the internal reporting mechanism. For example, postdated contracts (i.e., contracts taking effect up to 6 months after execution) are issued under the guarantee program. The yearend may intervene between the execution date and the effective date of coverage. In other cases, contracts executed late in one calendar year may not be picked up by the Controller in time to be included in the amount outstanding at the end of the year.

The following summary reconciles the variances since 1961 on a yearly basis: [In millions] 1961 1962 1962 1964 1965

(The information follows:) INTERNATIONAL EXECUTIVE SERVICE CORPS AND OTHER NONPROFIT ORGANIZATIONS PROVIDING TECHNICAL ASSISTANCE TO INDUSTRY IN THE LESS DEVELOPED COUNTRIES 

As of December 31, 1965, there were 54 technical service contract or grant agreements entered into by AID with nonprofit firms and associations, exclusive of universities. There appears to be only one organization, the Council for International Progress in Management, some of whose activities are in the same general area as IESC. Some other nonprofit organizations, the Brookings Institution and the Stanford Research Institute, have made studies in the past year under AID contracts to broadly assist private enterprise in some of the less developed countries. There is no standard overhead rate used in these technical service contracts. Charges for administrative services are negotiated case by case and generally definitions of direct and indirect costs reimbursable at different rates are established.

The Council for International Progress in Management has a number of contracts with AID for services to be performed in less developed countries. The 

Council plans and conducts seminars for industrial managers from private businesses in foreign countries, assists local management by providing management specialists and technical advisers. It provides investment counsel and production assistance as well as development planning and training programs. In 1965, its AID contracts totaled $1.022 million for which it was reimbursed a little over $100,000 for home office staff services.

The International Executive Service Corps, a new organization which completed its first full year of project activity in January 1966, received from AID for the calendar year 1965 the sum of $690,476.69 to carry on its program of direct assistance to private enterprise in developing countries. Of this amount, $188,575 was for salaries. Home office administrative charges totaled $76,774.18. 

Under the terms of the grant agreement with IESC, AID reimburses the Corps for specific percentages of approved project and nonproject costs. Through calendar year 1965, AID reimbursed 80 percent of IESC direct project costs and 75 percent of its indirect project costs. For the year beginning January 15, 1966, the percentages were reduced to 75 percent and 65 percent, respectively. Further reductions are anticipated as IESC receives increasing support from the American business community and reduces the starting up level of its operating costs. 

The IESC is a new approach to improving the management practices of private enterprises in the less developed countries. The idea of sending an experienced

FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT ACT OF 1966 

American businessman as an unpaid volunteer to assist a business in his line was first advanced by two members of Congress and a prominent American businessman. After a review by several departments of the Government and with congressional approval, AID made a grant to the newly formed IESC providing financial assistance for its early development. To speed the organization of 

TESC and make its program available on a worldwide basis the initial funding was substantial. There are some other nonprofit organizations, but not in the field of business, which also receive substantial financial assistance from AID. 

The Executive Corps program has been well received in 20 of the less developed countries and by the American business community. Its project level is continuing to accelerate and the direct business to business assistance which it provides appears to be serving a need in the less developed countries. 

Mr. HOAGLAND. There are many organizations, cooperatives, savings and loan organizations, who have contracts with us to furnish technical assistance. CIPM is a very valuable source of this kind of help whose contract has been extended for another year and which is carrying on some interesting work in connection with placing foreign students in American industry for a year following their graduate work in this country.

I don't have in mind a fixed number of organizations of this kind but I would be glad to furnish you with additional material of this kind.

Mr. HOAGLAND. The effort is to have an efficient organization managed by people who are accustomed to large responsibilities. The president, Frank Pace, Jr., has had extensive experience both in industry and government. I believe that salary is appropriate for him and the Government will get its money's worth.

Zablocki, the volunteers who go out on the project-

May I ask a few questions so we can get some information to see whether AID is actually justified in this particular field? It is my understanding that AID's contribution for fiscal year 1966 is estimated at one and a half million dollars for the International Executive 

Service Corps.

For the record, would you supply how much AID has contributed to any of the other voluntary nonprofit organizations that have been in the same field, whether there is any duplication, whether the International Executive Service Corps has entered into areas where other nonprofit organizations are active. I would like to have a justification for this entire program. 

Mr. HOAGLAND. We would be glad to furnish that. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Adair. 

Mr. ADAIR. Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. Hoagland, as you know, this committee has been very much interested for a number of years now in the area with which you are primarily concerned. I personally have a great many questions about the whole matter of extended risk guarantees, but rather than try to explore those in the committee here, which would be, I think, excessively time consuming, I would like to ask if you and I couldn't sit down sometime reasonably soon for a half hour and go in some depth into this whole question. 

Mr. HOAGLAND. I would be glad to do that. 

Mr. ADAIR. You are proposing that we strike out the date which we put in last year, December 1, 1965, and make this a matter of Presidential discretion in fact? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. Yes, sir.

Mr. ADAIR. Without having reached a decision in my own mind upon that point, I would just ask you whether or not you do not feel that to do this would remove another control which the Congress has over this program and put it in the hands of the Executive, if we just say in effect "Mr. President, you are entirely free to use your own discretion in this matter after 1966"? 

Do you have any thoughts upon that?

Mr. HOAGLAND. I think it is a matter of balancing what this particular amendment might do and how it is used against the general problem of the administration in determining what country to carry on an assistance program in. It is certainly correct that this shifting is a conversion of a mandatory requirement into a discretionary

to requirement. The measure it seems to me has been a useful one up this point. It is hard to tell what influence it has had on countries. 

I think it probably has had some influence in helping us to get the agreements we have. 

Mr. ADAIR. There has been testimony in previous years that that was the case. When we first fixed 1965, it was indicated that it might be a useful persuader.

Mr. HOAGLAND. Yes. 

Mr. ADAIR. When you came before us a year ago and said another year was necessary, without any opposition this committee wrote in the new date, and it is my feeling that we would continue to be quite willing to do that if you felt it necessary.

Mr. HOAGLAND. The ideal situation would be one in which this was always a possibility and a meaningful one, and the form in which that is done is something we would be quite open to suggestion about. think that putting it at a short period when it is going to come to its conclusion would terminate its effectiveness once we get to that date, then I think we would face the problem in these countries where private investment is not very likely.

In many situations the country has not been in business long enough to know quite what this amendment would do or how the guarantee program works. But our national interest in general would require that we maintain some forms of assistance in the country. The amendment we propose is designed to put us in that position. 

Mr. ADAIR. Have you read the article from the Columbia Journal of Trans-National Law headed "Policy and Politics-U.S. Guaranty 

Program"? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. Yes.

Mr. ADAIR. I don't want to go into that in detail. It is an interesting article. When we sit down to talk, I should like to discuss some of the matters set forth here. 

Mr. HOAGLAND. All right, sir. I wish I had had a chance to talk to the young gentlemen who wrote it before they wrote it. 

Mr. ADAIR. In reading that myself, and I suppose it came to all members of this committee, I think it must have been sent around to us, it came to my desk-in reading it myself, I discovered some ideas that I thought were somewhat novel at least. In response to a question addressed to you by Mrs. Bolton, I understood you to say that there has been a conversion of certain loans to grants. 

Did you so answer her?

Mr. HOAGLAND. No, although that might be implied by my offer to furnish information about that for the record. I do not know the facts, Mr. Adair. I am offering to furnish them. I do not know whether such conversions have occurred or not. 

Mr. ADAIR. Frankly I was a little surprised at your response. I, like you, Mrs. Bolton, am very interested in that reply.

Mr. HOAGLAND. I doubt very much that there have been. Yet I would not want to make that statement without checking the record. 

I think it would be very rare, but I have not looked to see whether there has been an instance of that kind.

Mr. ADAIR. In the proposed legislation you have suggested that the specific risk guarantees, or the risk guarantees be extended to 1973. Is that the proposal you are making?

Mr. HOAGLAND. There is no date with respect to the political risk guarantees. On those there is simply a ceiling amount. With respect to the extended risk guarantees, there is the date of 1973.

Mr. ADAIR. If your housing guarantees are proposed to be extended to 1971, why do you select different dates as to other terminations?

As people have already indicated, I have most serious doubts that you will get the 5-year authority requested, but I am interested in why you select different dates for this.

Mr. HOAGLAND. The section 211(b) (2) that deals with extended risk guarantees is also an authority that covers housing guarantees. To the extent that housing guarantees are authorized under 221(b)(2), the extension to 1973 would apply to them as well. As for the housing guarantees for Latin America, if the date is 1971, then this is simply a difference in practice that has carried on because of the way these two sections have been dealt with in the past and for no other reason. 

Mr. ADAIR. You must confess it is a little confusing to have two dates for two similar programs. In the little time I have left, I have some very specific questions, and these have been touched upon before. 

You are giving a combined war risk and expropriation coverage now at seven-eighths of 1 percent; is that correct? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. Yes, sir. 

Mr. ADAIR. What do you charge for the three specific risk coverages?

Mr. HOAGLAND. Before those changes were made, there was a charge of one-half of 1 percent for each coverage. People took out varying amounts and the net cost of the insurance wasn't 12 percent. 

It was somewhere down around 14 percent. 

Under the present proposal, under the new fee schedule there is an option to take the war and expropriation coverages as a package for seven-eighths of 1 percent, and convertibility insurance is available whether you take the other package or not at one-fourth of 1 percent instead of one-half. 

Mr. ADAIR. But you don't give them a three-way deal at 1 percent, say? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. That is right. 

Mr. ADAIR. Can you now?

Mr. HOAGLAND. It is not offered as a three-way deal. Convertibility insurance cost a quarter of a percent if you take it all by itself or in any other way. 

Mr. ADAIR. That would make 1% percent. This perhaps isn't an important matter, but it just crossed my mind, that you might want to make a package arrangement for those three coverages. 

Mr. HOAGLAND. This has been suggested and we have considered it. 

The difference, and the reason we didn't go all the way was that convertibility comes up every year as dividends and interest are paid and you still have the whole investment in place. 

If you are looking at expropriation or war, it is the investment in place that gets damaged. If your coverage dwindles away because of

convertibility payments, you have reduced your coverage but our coverage but you he you have the same exposure to the other two risks.

Mr. ADAIR. The chairman is giving me the eye here. May we have for the record a showing as to the amount of income that this program has produced, the payments both as to number and gross amounts and the present status of the funds? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. Yes, sir. I can say quickly there is about a $30 million buildup of premium income and a net zero loss. 

Mr. ADAIR. I would like to have that in some sort of tabular form. 

Mr. HOAGLAND. We will furnish it for you.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Hays. 

Mr. HAYS. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Mr. Hoagland, to what do you attribute the fact that the Latin 

American housing program is practically bogged down? Bureaucracy? Hospitality in the AID or what?

Mr. HOAGLAND. I think the difficulties in getting projects finished off have to do with the difficulty, the complications of handling housing projects in areas where institutions don't exist for doing these things in the effective routine way that they are done in this country and other countries that are used to handling projects. 

We also have the problem of relating the use of our authority to the maximum development of institutions in those countries that will let them do more housing and create a multiplier effect. Working this out adds complications, I must admit. 

Mr. HAYS. You use the FHA to process these, is that right? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. They participate very heavily.

Mr. HAYS. Why? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. Because we think they start off knowing more about it and have a group of people experienced in the field that would be able to do it better than our own staff do.

Mr. HAYS. Did you read the article in the Reader's Digest of April about the FHA?

Ju Mr. HOAGLAND. I have not seen that. 

Mr. HAYS. You better get hold of it because this is a national scandal, and if 10 percent of what they say is true, the only thing the FHA has is an experienced bunch of shakedown artists and thieves. They have repeatedly jacked up the price of land as much as 800 percent for some of their pets. They have repeatedly allowed people who had taken a profit and gone away and left them holding the bag to get another shot at them. And I think it is fair to say that in the case of Latin American housing they have dragged their feet and made it impossible, or next door to impossible, for anybody to get started on a project down there. 

Mr. HOAGLAND. I am not familiar with that article. 

Mr. HAYS. You ought to be familiar with the whole procedure. I don't know how many people-you have one of these big titles: "Assistant Administrator for Development Finance and Private Enterprise, Agency for International Development." I would assume from that long title you are interested in getting private enterprise into these fields. 

Mr. HOAGLAND. That is right.

Mr. HAYS. When you throw all the roadblocks in the world in front of them, you don't really expect to get them in there, do you? Answer that "yes" or "no" if you want to.

Mr. HOAGLAND. I think the answer is "No" to the question of whether we ought to or want to throw roadblocks in the way of getting these things done. We want to do them efficiently. We know we are not moving as speedily as the needs of investors require and we are doing our best to improve.

Mr. HAYS. Would you think it would be fair to say, Mr. Hoagland, that the AID is completely or almost completely resistant to private enterprise, because if private enterprise moves in they move out?

Mr. HOAGLAND. I would not say that would be an accurate statement. 

Mr. HAYS. That is a difference of opinion. I think it is a correct statement. I think the bulk of the people down in AID are interested in perpetuating this thing and anything that seems likely to diminish it or bring it to a close is resisted by them, covertly, overtly, and any other way they can resist it. I have been around here a long time. I have supported this program for a lot longer probably than I should have if I had really evaluated it. I am about to a place where I think that the whole structure down there, with the possible exception of the Director and a few other people, is interested in perpetuating themselves and keeping this thing going. You disagree with that? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. Yes, sir, I do.

Mr. HAYS. You have a right to your opinion but I think the facts will bear me out. We have heard over and over again-it is a little bit like Vietnam-I have heard for at least 18 years about what things they have done and incidentally, Mr. Hoagland, I am not blaming you for this but I asked this great new man they got in charge of Latin America 3 or 4 weeks ago to send me a letter telling me one single solitary project that had worked out, and I haven't heard from him 60-929-66-pt. 3-

yet, and I am never going to hear from him because he can't produce any.

I am talking about Mr. Lincoln Gordon, in case you are in any doubt about it.

Mr. HOAGLAND. It may be that he had quite a few that he wanted to tell you about Mr. HAYS. I only asked him to tell me about one. If I get a letter into my office asking for information I try to answer it at least the same week and probably the same day.

Mr. HOAGLAND. He may have, as I say, Mr. Hays, he may have wanted to tell you about more than one. My impression was that he did want to.

Mr. HAYS. I am going to make a speech on the floor, and the press will get it, saying up to date I haven't heard from him.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Whalley. 

Mr. WHALLEY. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. Hoagland, after completing an investment survey you say you guarantee new investments against risks of convertibility, war damage, revolution and insurrection. On the basis of these guarantees what has been the experience of the investors? First, what is the average return on the investment? Second, is the return greater than if the investment was made in the United States in the same type business? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. We haven't kept figures showing the investment experience of the group of investors who have our investment guarantee coverages as a separate group. Looking generally at the history of investment there has been this kind of history. The Department of Commerce figures show that the average rate of return on U.S. domestic manufacturing has risen in recent years to a level of about 13 percent. 

The rate of return on direct investment in Europe used to be very much higher than that. In 1959 it was up around 18 percent. It has come back down as our rates of return have gone up and they are fairly close to each other. In the developing countries the rates of return are presently running a small fraction, I would say 2 or 3 percent, below the average rates of return on direct investments in the United States or in Europe.

Mr. WHALLEY. In your opinion why wouldn't the investor invest his money in the United States if the return is less in other places? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. I think it is because, first of all, those are average figures and none of them think they are going to be the average. 

Beyond that Americans firms are looking to the future. They see the presence of 2 billion people in the rest of the world who are going to grow in their capacity to consume and use products manufactured someplace and they are actively going out to find those markets and to be sure as they develop the American producers are the ones who have them and not somebody else.

Mr. WHALLEY. In other words, they are making an investment both in the United States and these other countries.

Mr. HOAGLAND. Yes, sir. _ont Bo edt ette th 18 

Mr. WHALLEY. Thank you. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Farbstein. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. Hoagland, what is the reaction of the various countries where we have excess currencies when it comes to making loans to foreign investors of that local currency?

Mr. HOAGLAND. I think they have been cooperative with the Cooley loan program, which is the one we principally use to make loans to firms that are affiliated with American companies. The general use of these currencies is consistent with their desire to build up business activity. I think they can see the payrolls and general economic benefits of continuing business activity. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Does that apply to housing also?

Mr. HOAGLAND. I think there have been a few cases in which this kind of currency has been used to finance housing investment; yes. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Have you had any complaints from any individual who sought to go into some of these countries where excess currency rests and has met resistance by the country as far as housing projects are concerned? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. They haven't come to my attention. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Whose attention would they come to if they were made?

Mr. HOAGLAND. I think they would come to the attention of our operating offices. My office does not operate the housing programs. 

If they are seeking Cooley funds or local currency funds the direct contact would be either with our mission in the field or with the Regional Bureau Office in Washington to whom the mission reports. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Would you say that it is necessary to get cooperation of the government of a particular country in order to proceed with a project? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. Yes, sir. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Have you found instances, or would you have the opportunity of learning whether there were instances of resistance on the part of these governments to any projects proposed by American_corporations or companies?

Mr. HOAGLAND. I certainly would have that opportunity. If you would like me to undertake some kind of search of this kind I would be glad to.

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Do you know of any proposed projects that have been resisted by these foreign countries wherein we have excess local currency?

Mr. HOAGLAND. Do you mean housing projects 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Housing is one of them.

Mr. HOAGLAND. Yes- 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. I think there was quite a to-do about some company here that wanted to build a fertilizer plant in India.

FOREIGN ASSISTANCE AC ACT OF 1966 

Mr. HOAGLAND. I was going to refer to fertilizer investment in India. 

Particularly in fertilizer production, the problem is a live, current problem. It isn't so much Indian resistance to the construction of fertilizer plants. It is the problem of working out all the parts of the transaction. There is not complete satisfaction with the positions the Indian Government has taken on these things although they have made some very positive moves forward in the last 6 months.

Mr. FARBSTEIN. I also understand there was resistance by the Indian Government to proposed housing projects where local labor was going to be used, where local products would be used in building and the American company wanted to go down there with the building blocks. I would like for you to check if it is possible to learn how many applications for loans have been turned down by our Government for local excess currency where American firms have sought to engage in some industry within a foreign country. Also if you have any knowledge how many different projects have been resisted by any foreign governments where applications have been sought for consent; and if there has been resistance why has there been resistance by the foreign government, and if we have turned down any companies that sought to use excess currencies in foreign countries why have we done that? I would like to get a picture of whether or not the statements contained in your memorandum here that we have made $40 to $50 million or $90 million worth of local currency loans are actually so. Also I would like to get the names of the companies to whom these various loans were made and for what purposes they were made. And whether or not these are profitmaking companies or whether these are solely non-profit-making companies. I would like to know what is happening to all this Cooley money that has been building up and why it has been building up to the degree it has. 

You have a billion dollars around, probably a billion dollars in India and Pakistan and the United Arab Republic, hundreds of millions of dollars and nothing is being done, when I know there are American companies that want to go into these various countries and use some of those funds and they meet with resistance all the way down the line.

Mr. HOAGLAND. I would be glad to furnish the information that you have asked for but I would like to comment, if I may, Mr. Chairman, that of the funds that we have accumulated in the excess currency countries, by no means all of it, a small portion of it is available for financing of business ventures under the Cooley program. Even in that small proportion I believe we have more than enough funds available in these excess countries to handle the demand. 

I am not aware that any significant cutback has occurred in excess currency countries, because companies with good projects have not been able to go forward because of our lack of currency. We have done nearly $200 million of lending of this kind. I wish we had funds of this kind in countries other than these excess countries. The demand,

I believe, is being met except clearly for cases in which countries would be resisting the project, whether it is local currency financing or dollar financing.

In India, for example, and in other countries the country is concerned about whether the industry that is coming in is going to contribute to the solution of its own balance-of-payments problem. If the industry is one which is going to sell its products locally and not help the country export this creates a special problem for countries who have a severe balance-of-payments problem. I think we would be sympathetic with that since we are working with them trying to solve it. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. In what category would you put housing?

Mr. HOAGLAND. Housing is an activity that would not cause an inflow to the country of foreign exchange. The question the country has to resolve in connection with housing is what priorities it wants to apply in the allocation of its own resources. The currency is a claim on its resources and it has to decide whether its people need housing more than they need fertilizer, whether its people need housing more than they need something that will contribute to other improvements in their agriculture. This is a matter of great policy importance in any country and where the countries are severely underdeveloped it is a hard choice.

Mr. FARBSTEIN. In other words, every country has a veto as to whether or not any of that local currency can be used. We could keep on piling up zillions of it and it still could not be used, is that not a fact?

Mr. HOAGLAND. That is technically correct. It assumes that we have no voice or influence with the country as a result of our relations with them and the presence of an AID program and I think that restores the balance to an important extent. (The following information has been supplied for the record:)

COOLEY LOAN PROGRAM 

1. From January 1, 1962, through December 31, 1965, AID has authorized 

180 Cooley loans in the amount of $196 million (U.S. dollar equivalent). A tabulation of these loans, showing country, borrower, amount, industry, purpose, and authorization date, is set forth in table A. 

2. In the period January 1, 1962, to February 18, 1966, AID received a total of 135 applications that did not result in Cooley loans being made. A tabulation of these applications, showing country, industry and purpose, amount requested, and the reason the loan was not made, is set forth in table B. In preparing this tabulation AID has defined an application as a submission containing the following information: A specific request for a Cooley loan in a specific amount; identification of the putative borrower; a description of the project to be financed; where eligibility depends on U.S. affiliation, the identification of a presumably eligible U.S. business firm; where it does not, a description of how the export of U.S. agricultural products would be increased; and identification in reasonable detail, by amounts and sources, of the other capital to be invested in the project.

3. No Cooley loan applications have been fully formally disapproved by the 

Indian Government. When a loan proposal includes features that may be contrary to that Government's policy with regard to Cooley loans, it is the practice of AID to discuss (or request the prospective applicant to discuss) the proposal informally with the Indian Government in order to get its concur rence before an application is prepared. Normally the Indian Government would question the use of Cooley funds for working capital, loans to U.S. companies for purchase of equity stock, projects involving a debt-equity ratio of more than 2 to 1, projects in industries not considered by that Government to be of a developmental nature, and projects for which local financing is available on comparable or appropriate terms. 

4. With regard to housing, it is our understanding that the Government of 

India has not generally favored housing projects with United States or other foreign participation because it has not been persuaded that the services provided by the foreign interests-generally know-how, since labor and material are provided locally-have been worth the foreign exchange cost of royalties and dividends, etc., payable to the foreign interests. Rather it believes (or has believed in relation to the proposals presented to it) that adequate knowhow in the construction of housing can be procured from Indian sources and paid for in local currency, without any drain on its foreign exchange resources. 

Because of this view, no housing project proposals in India have matured into applications for Cooley or other U.S. loans. We do not know how many housing proposals by U.S. interests have been presented to, and received unfavorable consideration by, the Government of India.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Gross. 

Mr. GROSS. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Mr. Hoagland, in connection with Mr. Zablocki's questions concerning the employment of so-called nonprofit organizations, do you employ consultants? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. The Agency does employ consultants; yes, sir. 

Mr. GROSS. Would you be good enough to provide for the record a list of these consultants for the past 3 years and the amounts paid to them?

Mr. HOAGLAND. I think that would be a substantial list, Mr. Congressman. Do you mean to include contract services as well as consultants as such? Consultants generally are individuals, is that what you had in mind?

Mr. GROSS. You have already been asked for the information concerning the employment of so-called nonprofit organizations. 

Yes, I would like to know who you are employing as consultants in addition to the staffs that are permanently employed, yours and others, in connection with your operation. Is this too much to ask? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. No, sir; I don't believe it is.

Mr. GROSS. Let's find out how far beyond the permanent staff you go. (The information requested is as follows:)
