Mr. GROSS. You are encouraging U.S. private investment abroad. 

What effect does this have on the balance-of-payments situation? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. The present policy we are pursuing is that the encouragement of private U.S. investment in developing countries is something we would continue to do even if we attempt to restrict private investment in the industrialized countries for the sake of our balance of payments. The direct capital flows to the developing countries as a result of private investment were $803 million in 1965 and $578 million in 1964, not counting reinvested earnings which would add a couple of hundred million. 

Mr. GROSS. Mr. Hoagland, since I have other questions to ask, and 

I would like to ask them, can you say whether this has an adverse effect upon the balance-of-payments situation? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. It has a short-run bookkeeping adverse effect, and a longrun plus effect. 

Mr. GROSS. I would like to go into that further, because I don't agree with you on that. The Watson Committee urged the United States to give full support to the principle of an investment code under international sponsorship. AID commented that no significant support for the OECD draft convention-that is, for the protection of foreign property has yet appeared among the developing countries. Why has there been no support among the developing countries for this code? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. I think it is because they are very concerned about their power to advance their affairs and handle investment without the participation of outside judges of their internal law. In most of these situations what they are concerned about is that they anticipate the way they adopt laws and reach judgments in their courts are going to be questioned internationally, and they think that as long as they are offering nondiscriminatory treatment for foreign investment with their local investment that that is all that ought to be asked of them.

Mr. GROSS. They are perfectly willing to take American dollars, are they not, without joining in establishing a code or assuring-giving us any reasonable assurance of safety of investment?

Mr. HOAGLAND. They have entered into other kinds of agreements with us such as the bilateral guarantee agreement, which is cooperative. 

Mr. GROSS. This leads to the question. We have dished out $130 billion or more in foreign aid around the world, and this we were told through the years was for the purpose of creating a favorable climate for American investment; to make American investments safe abroad.

How much longer are you going to continue this program, or is it to go on indefinitely and forever?

Mr. HOAGLAND. I think this program is for more than to make 

American investment safe abroad, as you appreciate, Mr. Congressman. It is to make a contribution to the peace and security of the world, and to build nations that will be partners and neighbors that we can get along with and be both secure and prosperous with in the long run. The program is designed and planned to work itself out of business. I cannot tell you how long that is going to take, but that is the purpose of it.

Mr. GROSS. Do you have any more situations like that in London where there was a guarantee to protect the Knott Hotels Corp.?

FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 

557 1966 Mr. HOAGLAND. I don't know that situation, Mr. Congressman. 

Mr. GROSS. This was some years ago. I have never been able to understand why there was any necessity for any guarantee program in England.

Mr. HOAGLAND. This refers, I assume, to the period immediately after World War II when we had convertibility insurance and we were at that time responsive to the dollar gap in Europe and trying to reconstruct that area and encourage American industry and investment to go there, in much the same spirit as is involved in these programs. 

Mr. GROSS. Are the multilateral organizations, such as the U.N. and 

OAS to assist the development and improvement of local financial institutions and support of private enterprise in the less developed countries?

Mr. HOAGLAND. Specialized agencies of the U.N. carry on technical assistance programs that would contribute particularly to industrial techniques, and the improvement of development banks and institutions of this kind that would service the private sector. 

The World Bank is particularly strong in this respect in financing and improving intermediate credit institutions that would service private borrowers. 

Mr. GROSS. Are you saying that is true as to both the U.N. and the OAS?

Mr. HOAGLAND. I think it would be less true of them than the 

World Bank. 

Mr. GROSS. I would think so. Do our food-for-peace agreements with all countries include-I believe that has been brought up under the Cooley programs by Mr. Farbstein-do they include all the Cooley loan provisions and, if not, why not?

Mr. HOAGLAND. It is part of the negotiation in every situation. 

During the past year out of, I would say, 10 food-for-peace agreements, about 5 were with small African countries in which very little allocation for Cooley was possible simply because there was no demand, but an allocation was made. The only ones in which there were none made was in the Congo, in Vietnam, and in Afghanistan where there was no demand for the funds. Unless there is no demand, there is some percentage set aside. 

Mr. GROSS. Are the British still charging the going rate of interest in England on their loans to foreign countries?

Mr. HOAGLAND. They have this past year made a change in policy which has resulted in loans which in some cases are longer in term and lower in interest rates than ours. They have some no-interest loans now to developing countries from England. 

Mr. GROSS. I cannot help but ask a question, perhaps you can't answer it, but where do the British get the money to make no-interest loans, since we are propping up the pound sterling. 

Mr. Chairman, that is all.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Roybal. 

Mr. ROYBAL. Mr. Hoagland, I am fast coming to the conclusion that your program in Latin America looks a lot better on paper than it does on the scene. In a recent visit to Latin America I noticed that AID people lived in the best districts in whatever country they happen to be assigned to. It appears to me that they don't seem to care

too much about the slum dweller and that their programs do not really include the people who live in the slum community. This I saw in 

Peru, for example, and even some in Chile. 

What specifically is being done to provide housing for the people in the lowest income brackets-not only in Peru and Chile, but throughout Latin America?

Mr. HOAGLAND. The housing program in the form enacted by the 

Congress last year shifts as you know from a pilot demonstration type of program to a program authorized to go into four particular kinds of projects. One of them is the type of project which will finance housing for low-income families for a maximum sales price of $2,500. The hope is that we can get those units down to as low as $1,500. At the same time, in terms of what the general interests of the slum dweller is, and of the most severely impoverished portion of the population, one of the most difficult choices you have to make is, given the fact that we cannot and would not want to simply support a country's economy, we want to use our resources in the way that makes a maximum contribution and moves them to make the maximum contribution, and there is a hard choice to where to put it. If we spread the money around in dollar bills to the poorest people, we wouldn't accomplish anything. We have to tackle the problems comprehensively, and that is the way it is being done where our interest is strong enough.

Mr. ROYBAL. What I was referring to specifically was a program that I saw in its inception in which the people in the slum areas were forming cooperatives. They started making their own homes, using indigenous materials, and their own labor. The house would definitely not cost $2,500 because these people couldn't afford to pay $2,500 in their lifetime. This to me is the type of program that we should be advancing in certain Latin American countries. 

What is being done insofar as AID is concerned in regard to a program of this kind where they, themselves, participate in building their own houses, in providing their own sewage and good sanitary conditions?

As I say, I have seen a pilot project of a private group that is doing this, and I would want to know what AID is doing in this regard. 

Mr. HOAGLAND. I agree self-help housing is an excellent mechanism. 

This is the kind of thing that has to be generated from within, from the energy and the initiative of the people concerned. But the Inter- 

American Development Bank in particular is actively assisting in loans and other activities to encourage this kind of housing. The forms of housing guarantees that we have been discussing are by definition to guarantee the cost of construction. Other programs to encourage credit unions and savings and loans and to encourage housing that might be put together by cooperatives are handled largely through technical assistance where we sent advisers to help groups of this kind who have this kind of energy.

Mr. ROYBAL. This again makes this thing very complicated to that individual. He is not interested in the finances of a credit union. Nor does he understand the finances of a savings and loan. He is interested in his immediate problem. What is AID doing to motivate these people to form their own cooperatives so at a very minimum they can get away from their atmosphere of poverty and squalor? יין

Mr. HOAGLAND. We have arrangements with cooperative organizations in this country to support the activities of the respresentatives of the cooperative movements in the cities of Latin America, who are teaching this concept as you describe it. We place a high value on it. 

Mr. ROYBAL. Do you have such a person in Peru; Lima, for example?

Mr. HOAGLAND. I am confident there is such a person. 

Mr. ROYBAL. I didn't have the pleasure of meeting this gentleman, if he was there. It could be that he just happened not to be in town. 

I saw no evidence, however, of any activity in this direction. 

Mr. HOAGLAND. It is more likely that he might have been in the back country.

Mr. ROYBAL. That is where I visited. I visited the back country. I visited I think all of the slum areas of Peru and I saw no evidence of AID doing this. In fact, the Peace Corps was instrumental in making funds available so that the people in these slum areas were providing their own sewage system. They were doing it on a voluntary basis. each man was devoting so much time a week to this project. From what little funds were available the campesino provided his share and was matched by funds from the Peace Corps. 

What type of program have you developed that will put this concept into effect throughout Latin America? I think this is the only way you can reach the people who cannot possibly afford to pay $2,500 in their lifetime?

Mr. HOAGLAND. As I say, it is through the activities of representatives of American cooperative organizations and through community development programs, the financing of rural community water systems, and the rural educational systems where the people of the areas have to combine and work together to support the activity that is involved. 

Peru is a good example of where an extensive community development program is being undertaken, particularly in the rural areas. 

Mr. ROYBAL. You are talking about something that I am sure they don't understand. They are not knowledgeable nor are they really interested in high financing procedures. They are interested in someone from your Agency going in with them, motivating them to help themselves, organizing them on a so-called grassroots level so that together they can accomplish what they have not been able to do throughout the generations; that is, get out of their slum conditions. They are not asking for a five-bedroom home; they are, however, asking for something better than they have had before. I think this can be done if the AID program goes into this type of program development in various areas throughout Latin America. 

I sincerely recommend that you look into this type of community development program very seriously, and see if something can be done to apply this concept. 

Mr. HOAGLAND. Thank you.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Thomson. 

Mr. THOMSON. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. Hoagland, on page 4 of your statement you discuss the increase in the specific risk guarantee investment program. Can you tell us whether there is any relationship between the amount of guarantee

investment in these countries, and the amount of other forms of aid that are provided? In other words, does aid decrease in a country in proportion to the specific risk guarantee investment increase?

Mr. HOAGLAND. It does not, Mr. Congressman. This figure does not represent assets going into the country. It represents contingent liability in the event of certain particular kinds of loss. It is important to point out that the figure on value of coverage is very much larger than the amount of investment, because if a single dollar of investment is covered against three risks that shows up as $3 of coverage. The amount of investment covered is anywhere from 30 to 50 percent in various years of the amount of coverage issued. 

The answer to your question is "No."

Mr. THOMSON. The coverage does represent some investment and some activity in that country? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. Yes, sir. Mr. THOMSON. There is no relationship between an increase in investment activity and the amount of aid that this country might continue to supply?

Mr. HOAGLAND. The examination of the country's needs includes a consideration of the amount of private investment from all sources that can be expected in the country. This is part of the analysis of how it is progressing, and what its needs might be, and what the chances are of doing something constructive with aid financing. 

These figures do not represent investment all in a single year; therefore, it is not a figure that can be used in examining the various sources of capital that might be coming to a country in any particular year. 

Mr. THOMSON. Then this investment program doesn't provide any hope that there can be a decrease in the amount of aid that might be needed in that country?

Mr. HOAGLAND. I would not want it to be construed that way because as investment increases, and as it becomes apparent, as it did in Taiwan and Greece, that a country can attract private investment, this is the very kind of progression that will cause us to conclude that aid can be phased out as it has been in those countries. This figure represents a contribution to the process of encouraging private investment, and as private investment responds and as more of it flows, this has a direct part in the conclusion that we hope to come to with every country, that private or other sources of capital are moving for natural causes and it would not be necessary to continue an aid program. 

Mr. THOMSON. Then you are saying that it does contribute but it is very remote?

Mr. HOAGLAND. I would say that it does contribute, and that it is intangible, but it is not remote. It is direct. It just isn't arithmetically applied except in the analysis of the country's total economy every year.

Mr. THOMSON. In the analysis that you have made on page 2 you say you have an analysis of investment conditions in certain countries. 

Do you have such an analysis for India, for example? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. The bottom of page 2, did you say? 

Mr. THOMSON. Yes; where you say you have just completed a new series of studies and they have been collected for the purpose of dissemination. I thought if you had one for India I would like to receive it.

Mr. HOAGLAND. The material that is referred to there is the work done to develop a special collection of data for southeast Asia, and India is not included in that work. This reference to the current analysis of investment laws and conditions does not include India. This included the countries in the region of particularly the Vietnam region and the surrounding countries that are the subject of the southeast 

Asia development effort. 

Mr. THOMSON. Refer to your preceding paragraph and you say you have a catalog containing some 1,200 studies. Then you say the fourth edition, the index released in January of this year, lists over 

1,600 titles. Do you have any on India in that group? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. Yes, sir. I am sure there are studies outstanding of investment laws and conditions in India. There are several good works written by Mr. Kust and Mr. Surrey that go over this ground, and I would be glad to send you either a reference to some of them or what we think are the best ones.

Mr. THOMSON. I would like to have some of the best ones. 

Mr. HOAGLAND. Yes, sir. 

Mr. THOMSON. Are you requesting a 5-year authorization in your part of the foreign aid program? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. Yes, sir.

Mr. THOMSON. I notice that among all of the outstanding countries that you list in your remarks you mention Korea and you mention 

Brazil and others, but you don't happen to mention India. Is that because we are having difficulties in that country? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. The list of countries, the places where I have listed countries naturally were for various purposes-- 

Mr. THOMSON. Ön page- 

Mr. HOAGLAND. On page 5-excuse me.

Mr. THOMSON. On page 3 you mention Brazil, the hybrid seed plant in Brazil.

Mr. HOAGLAND. Yes. That is an interesting case because the company that investigated the hybrid seed business in Brazil under our investment survey program decided to make the investment in Brazil and then went ahead and undertook another survey in India. 

Mr. THOMSON. They didn't like India, did they?

Mr. HOAGLAND. I don't know what their answer has been. This is the Cargill Co. I understand the survey is underway at this time. 

It is certainly true that the conditions of entry for companies considering investment in India are troublesome at the present time. 

Mr. THOMSON. On page 6 you mention a fertilizer plant is going into Korea. But recent press stories by the administration indicate that is one of the primary concerns in India. You don't mention India in that category either.

Mr. HOAGLAND. There are some very active negotiations now. And of the extended risk guarantees that I referred to, things that may be coming up in the next few months, two of them have to do with possible fertilizer plants, one in Brazil and one in India. 

There are I would say five or six fertilizer investments under discussion with India and this is a field in which it seems to me the Indian Government has been facing its problem and making some changes that give us some reason to think that they will encourage private investment. After all, some time ago they wouldn't permit

fertilizer production to be undertaken in the private sector at all. 

They now will.

Mr. THOMSON. Does your index or your analysis point up these problems? When you analyze a country do you list the problems that exist or do you just list the possible benefits?

Mr. HOAGLAND. This is intended to be reliable information. When a study of a country is done we try to be sure it is a good one before it goes in the index. At the same time what you are describing and the feel for the situation that you are reflecting depends on current information. Studies that go into indexes do have the problem of talking about something that happened, inevitably, 6 months ago at least. 

This kind of information, the feel for current conditions in a country is something that has to be acquired from other sources. 

Mr. THOMSON. Thank you very much.

It shouldn't be left obscure that the rate of action in the program is currently being established by reference to our balance of payments.

(The information follows:) PROCESSING TIME-SPECIFIC RISK GUARANTEE APPLICATIONS 

A recent study of all contracts signed during calendar year 1965 and the first quarter of calendar year 1966 revealed the following information concerning the lapse of time between the date the FGA was received and the date that the contract was executed.¹ [In days] 

Median time. 

Average time.. 1965 

First quarter, 1966 172 249 188 228 Complete studies have not been made on contracts issued in prior years although samples have been taken periodically because of the realization that the time involved in negotiating and processing is of major importance to investors. A sample of roughly 22 percent of the contracts written in 1963 indicated a lapse of 271 days between the receipt of FGA and execution of the contract. Another sample taken in 1964 which took into consideration 14 percent of the contracts written in that year showed a lapse of 292 days between receipt of FGA and execution of the contract. 

Even though these figures reflect a reduction in processing time over the past few years, it is still difficult to conclude on the basis of statistics alone whether the delays encountered are caused by the investor or AID. Studies indicate the responsibility for delay varies from case to case and at present the matter is under close examination in order to isolate areas within the agency where "snags" tend to occur. Efforts are also being made to enable investors to provide required information promptly and accurately. These efforts include a recently revised edition of the Investment Guarantees handbook which supplies detailed information on the program and a carefully designed application form which seeks to extract from the investor all of the lements pertinent to the granting of investment guarantees.

Several steps, including the filing of an application for a waiver letter, usually take place before the FGA is received but earnest negotiations normally do not begin until this key requirement is met.

(The information follows:) PROPOSED AMENDMENT TO SECTION 620 (1) 

TO The effectiveness of section 620(1) as a persuasive element in inducing countries to enter into an agreement to institute the investment guarantee program for inconvertibility and expropriation lies in its potential use. Once the termination date is reached, the effectiveness is lost. Extension of the termination date for relatively short period tends to diminish effectiveness. 

The proposed amendment directs the President to consider termination of aid if any country fails to have such an agreement by December 31, 1966. Consideration will have to be given to termination of AID assistance after careful assessment of all of the U.S. policy objectives surrounding such assistance-but bearing in mind the strong policy objective of making such agreements to facilitate U.S. private investment in the country involved. 

The proposed amendment is designed to put the United States in a position which would allow aid to flow to those countries which had not instituted the guarantee program before December 31, 1966, where and when it would be in the national interest to do so.

(The information follows:) AID PROGRAM LOANS AND EXIMBANK LOANS 

The lending resources of the Export-Import Bank (Eximbank) and AID are used in a complementary fashion. Export-Import Bank long-term loans are normally related to a specific project. They are repayable in dollars and finance only U.S. dollar costs. Repayment terms on Eximbank loans are those appropriate to the project. AID program loans assist in meeting the overall foreign exchange requirements of the borrowing country. They are also repayable in dollars and are used predominantly for the procurement of U.S. goods and services. Repayment terms are geared to the debt servicing capacity of the borrowing country.

Before making program loans, AID is required by the Foreign Assistance Act of 1961 to take into account the availability of financing from other sources. This includes financing available from the Eximbank, as well as other national and international lending agencies.

The lending activities of the Eximbank and AID are closely coordinated. The 

President and Chairman of the Board of the Eximbank is a member of the interagency Development Loan Committee, which is chaired by the Administrator of AID. Both aid and the Eximbank are represented on the National Advisory 

Council on International Monetary and Financial Policies. In addition, an 

AID staff member attends, on behalf of the Department of State and AID, the regular meetings of the Eximbank's Board of Directors. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Frelinghuysen. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. Hoagland, I would like to ask you whether your responsibilities would be affected in any way if there were a 5-year authorization for this program instead of the present year-by-year basis, and if so in what way would your responsibilities be affected?

Mr. HOAGLAND. In connection with the investment guarantee authorities I think there is some modest improvement in the probabilities that investors will go ahead with plans if they know the program is going to be in existence for a significant number of years. 

In the past, this has been covered by making the authority stretch somewhat in amount beyond what is immediately required, and I would say that the 5-year program would have a tendency in this direction but not be critical.

The aspect in which I think there would be a real gain has to do with the effort we make to make performance in terms of strengthening the private sector a part of our evaluation of a country's self-help performance.

When we are trying to work out the terms of aid and work with. a country on an examination of the things it does to justify our going forward with assistance, we want to be able to show that we are ready to respond if they are ready to take the actions we think are necessary. 

Among the things we are in a position to work on are the conditions for private investment. Our effectiveness in improving the conditions for private investment will relate to the credibility with

which we come before the country saying we are going to undertake some aid activities if it seems promising in this country and in our interest. And among the things we are concerned with is how you handle private investment in your own private sector. 

When the aid program is on a 1-year authorization basis and we are trying to influence their policies in connection with private investment, we have a weaker weapon than if it is a 5-year authorization. 

In that respect

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. In what sense? Why are you not in a position to be persuasive in showing that you want to help if they can meet the conditions?

Mr. HOAGLAND. Because what we are talking to them about is ndamental changes in conditions and in policy that are to last for a considerable period of time.

If it is unclear whether we are going to be in a position to respond with AID assistance, and this would not be in the form of a comnitment, if it is unclear how long we are going to be there, during the time that some kind of improvement is needed, we are not as persuasive as if we are in a position to work with them on a development plan over some period of time.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Why is it unclear? In any event the money is not going to be available. I could understand your argument if the money was going to be available over a long period of time. If the money has to come from Congress on an annual basis, how is there more clarity by having a long-term authorization for these programs? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. Unless there is an authorization there is no basis for appropriation.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Of course not. I am not arguing that. I understand that. This has nothing to do with their appreciation of whether we are going to help them once they make these changes in their own economy.

Mr. HOAGLAND. I think it has to do with their evaluation of whether the intentions of the U.S. Government to continue this kind of assistance are clearly enough set forth so that it is in their interest to cooperate with us Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. The intention is basically whether we are going to provide the money. That is not clearer because the programs are authorized. All you are doing is misleading the recipient countries if by lengthening the authorization you suggest this is changing this picture. It is not changing it. 

On the basis of the past performance there is a pretty good indication that we are going to follow through with any commitment we make, even though we don't provide either money for a 10-year period or an authorization for a long period.

Mr. HOAGLAND. On past performance we have had greatest success in working with countries on their policies in several connections with negotiation for development loans, and in countries [security deletion] there have been times when we were holding back on this lending as a result of this very kind of dialog. But we were working from a base of a 5-year authorization for that kind of lending. Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. We are not suggesting cutting back and changing that aspect at all. I am talking about the changes being proposed now.

Mr. HOAGLAND. If it is proposed that the authorization for such things as development lending and Alliance for Progress lending be something other than 5 years there is a different pattern than the pattern that was instituted in 1962 when there was a 5-year authorization. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. I am not suggesting that we should abandon what we have already adopted as policy. What I am talking about are the changes beyond present policy. 

Mr. HOAGLAND. In general it just seems to me that without this becoming a commitment, if we have a multiyear 5-year form of authorization it is clear that we are moving toward carrying on a program although it is not a bit clear and never would be committed about what the extent of it might be. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. If it is not a commitment I don't see how it is of any particular value to the recipient country. If it is a commitment it would seem to me to be very impressive evidence that the country should change in order to meet our requirement. I don't see how anything less than that is of any great value one way or another as far as our persuasiveness is concerned. 

If I might change the subject, the Watson Committee had a recommendation that AID should actively promote the development of management schools and vocational institutions. As I understand it AID has been supporting a number of these schools. Also there is a feeling in AID that a broader effort is required. AID also has stated that our successes and failures are now being evaluated. I wonder if you could give us an indication of what the problems are that you have encountered in supporting these schools and institutions? What is the score so far as successes and failures are concerned? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. The problems would include these: A choice has to be made whether to furnish this training in the United States in schools here or in the country itself. When you bring management and business administration students to this country for training one of the big risks is that they will never go home, that they will come here, be trained, their skills will be appreciated, they would like it and want to stay.

Another problem would be that if they are trained here they are trained at a level of sophistication for a type of industry which really is not what they have to go back to if they do go home, and that the kind of business training we have here has to be adapted and altered to really be suitable for people who are going to be handling business responsibilities in developing countries. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. If I might interrupt, the recommendation of the Watson Committee was that these institutions should be in the developing countries, not to bring the foreigners here. Have we been supporting an improvement of the institutions in the developing countries and if so what are the problems?

Mr. HOAGLAND. What I was describing was the problems we had encountered before this recommendation came along and it seems persuasive to me that the thrust of this should be in the country. It may be that the teachers for these schools would have to have some training in this country. At the present time the International Center for the 

Advancement of Management Education at Stanford is considering the possibility of shifting its base from this country to some such country

I would as India in order to focus more directly on the local scene. hope we would be in a position to support that, although no decision has been made. There have been successes I think in the Haile Selassie niversity in Ethiopia where a good school of business administraon with our support has been started and is going on. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. This is something we had not been supporting ntil the Watson recommendation?

Mr. HOAGLAND. No. The Watson recommendations were just last summer, and I would say in terms of new projects since then no new school of business administration has been launched since that. It bakes more time than that to organize a project. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. I am not referring to the launching of new rograms. I mean the AID assistance. 

Mr. HOAGLAND. AID assistance most recently in this field has gone an investigation in Tunisia of the AID assistance that might go into the strengthening of the business school in Tunisia. The same in 

Turkey. We are working on a project to really join with the Ford 

Foundation and other organizations doing considerable work of this And in a business and management institution in Turkey. The work of George Lodge in Central America and other places has also received pport since that recommendation and is again a placement of a business school teaching capability in the developing country. His work has to do with using the Harvard Business School method in schools cated, I think in his case it is, the one I have particularly in mind is in Nicaragua.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. You haven't really given us an indication of what the successes have been or what the failures have been. Hasn't 

AID done enough in this area for you to be specific?

Mr. HOAGLAND. In terms of successes and failures since the Watson 

Committee report we surely would not. In terms of over time I don't 

Think we have a sufficient evaluation of all the different things we have done to give you a score except to point out the problems I referred to nd the sense we have that the Watson Committee is right, that the base of operations would best be in the developing country with occasional training of teachers here in this country. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. I don't know what problems you refer to xcept problems that we are not talking about; that is, bringing the 

Toreigners to this country for training. I meant the institutions- 

Mr. HOAGLAND. The problem at the other end is that in many countries a teacher from the United States on this subject has to get through a cultural barrier.

I think it can be done. In terms of what the problems are it has een our experience that it is difficult in a country like Pakistan to Bring experienced teachers of American business into that culture and hat business environment and start them teaching something that is going to be received effectively by business people or business school students in the country.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. One last brief question, Mr. Chairman. The 

International Executive Service Corps, you have some nice things to say about that program. How is the financing now done? I see you re hoping there is going to be less need for AID financing. How is it done? These executives primarily are retired individuals, very well qualified, who receive some remuneration for their basically volun-

tary assignments as I understand it. Is that right? Is AID presently financing the necessary costs of such participation by these executives? 

Mr. HOAGLAND. AID is now financing 75 percent of the project costs and 65 percent of the nonproject costs of the whole operation. Th excludes from costs any headquarters salaries over $26,000 a yea The volunteer, when he goes out, is paid an amount that is intende to cover his expenses which approximates $1,000 a month. He is not paid a salary. He is, as you say, usually a highly qualified close to retirement or retired executive, although there have been a few and I hope will be more middle management people going out this way. 

The business they go into pays the International Executive Service Corps a fee for this man's services which may vary from one hundred or a few hundred dollars a month up to perhaps a thousand dollars a month. It is a fraction of what a U.S. salary for the man would be but the purpose is to be sure that the business pays something for what it gets.

There is also financing by contributions from the business con munity. We are looking forward to a greater participation by the business community, and we expect there will be fundraising efforts made sometime during the fall of 1966. Following that we will hap a better reading on the extent to which the business community wil be prepared to assume the full burden of financing this activity. 

This is our target. We do not yet know whether we are going to reach it.
